Asymptotics of integral points, equivariant compactifications and equidistributions for homogeneous spaces
Runlin Zhang

Abstract
. Let 𝐔 \bm{\mathrm{U}} be a homogeneous variety over ℚ \mathbb{Q} of a linear algebraic group. Let 𝒰 \mathcal{U} be an integral model and assume the existence of infinitely many integral points. Then one would like to give an asymptotic count of integral points of bounded height with the help of some height function. In many cases, with the help of measure rigidity of unipotent flows, we reduce this problem to one on equivariant birational geometry. For instance, we show that if 𝐆 \bm{\mathrm{G}} and 𝐇 \bm{\mathrm{H}} are both connected, semisimple, simply connected and without compact factors, then 𝐆 / 𝐇 \bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}} is strongly Hardy-Littlewood with respect to some height function. We also show that when 𝐇 \bm{\mathrm{H}} is “large” in 𝐆 \bm{\mathrm{G}} and both 𝐆 \bm{\mathrm{G}} and 𝐇 \bm{\mathrm{H}} are connected, reductive and without nontrivial ℚ \mathbb{Q} -characters, the asymptotic of integral points is the same as the volume asymptotic up to a constant for every equivariant height. Three concrete examples with explicit heights are also provided to illustrate our approach.



1.
Introduction

Given a set of polynomials with integral coefficients, Diophantine problems concern the existence of some integral solution or infinitely many solutions.
Assuming there are infinitely many solutions, however, one can still ask for an asymptotic count provided there is a height function.

In this paper we consider the special case when the equations have rich symmetries: their complex solutions allow a transitive action of some linear algebraic group.
There are many works on when an integral solution exists in this context (see
[
BR95
,
CTX09
,
EV08
]
for a small sample of research).
Assuming that integral solutions do exist,
the question remained is to count them and we are mainly interested in the case when there are infinitely many.
It turns out that the set of integral solutions has a nice structure: it is acted on by an arithmetic subgroup and decomposes into finitely many orbits.
So naturally one wants to find the asymptotic count of a single orbit first.
It was observed by
[
DRS93
]
that such a question is related to equidistribution of homogeneous measures on a special type of homogeneous spaces: arithmetic quotients of real points of a linear algebraic groups. Ergodic theoretic methods then kick in and solve the problem in many cases
[
EM93
,
EMS96
]
.
It is also realized that the height function one use to count the solutions matters.

The purpose of the present paper is to continue the study in
[
EMS96
,
Zha21
]
via unipotent flows, that is, the celebrated work of Ratner
[
Ra91
]
, and reduces the counting problem in many cases to geometric questions about the height function.
In the remaining part of this introduction, we will present some theorems as well as problems one encounters in this process.

As far as counting integral points on homogeneous varieties is concerned, there are many existing works. Besides those already mentioned, some relevant ones are
[
BO12
,
CLT12
,
Cho24
,
MG14
,
GN12
,
GOS09
,
HT03
,
KK18
,
Mau07
,
OS14
,
Sha00
,
SZ19
,
TBT13
,
Zha19
]
, touching on different aspects of the question.

1.1.
Hardy–Littlewood varieties

The notion of strongly Hardy–Littlewood varieties is introduced in
[
BR95
]
(compare
[
Bir62
]
) for affine varieties.
Such varieties are defined by certain nice local-to-global properties.
To simplify discussions, we specialize to the case when
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
is isomorphic to
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
for some semisimple linear algebraic group
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and a semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
. In particular, we assume that
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
.

1.1.1.
Integral points

Let
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
be an affine homogeneous variety over
\mathbb{Q}
and
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})
be the topological space of adelic points. Given an open compact subset
\mathscr{K}_{f}\subset\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q},f})
of finite adeles and a connected component
\mathscr{K}_{\infty}
of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
, one define the set of
\mathscr{K}
-integral points
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{\mathscr{B}}(\mathbb{Z})
to be the intersection of
\mathscr{K}:=\mathscr{K}_{\infty}\times\mathscr{K}_{f}
with the diagonal embedding to
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})
in the set of adeles.

1.1.2.
Tamagawa measures

Let
\omega
be a nonvanishing top-degree differential form on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
over
\mathbb{Q}
. It is actually
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-invariant and induces measures
\omega_{p}
on each
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
(
\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}:=\mathbb{R}
) and, in fact, the products over finite
p
’s induce a measure
\omega_{f}
on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q},f})
.
By the product formula and the triviality of global regular invertible functions on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
, the measure
\omega_{f}\otimes\omega_{\infty}
is independent of the choice of
\omega
.

1.1.3.
The definition

Let
l:\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a proper function and

B_{R}:=\left\{x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R}),\;l(x)\leq R\right\}.

We say that
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
is a strongly Hardy-Littlewood variety (with respect to
l
) if for any choice of
\mathscr{K}
as above,

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\#\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{\mathscr{K}}(\mathbb{Z})\cap B_{R}}{\omega_{f}\otimes\omega_{\infty}(B_{f}\times B_{R})}=1.

In
[
BR95
]
,
l
is induced from an embedding of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
into some affine space
\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{n}
.

1.1.4.
Theorems

Now, assume further that
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
are both connected and simply connected. Also assume that the real points of every nontrivial
\mathbb{Q}
-factor of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is noncompact. Note that we have assumed
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
.
The following is
[
BR95
, Theorem 0.3]
:

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that for any congruence subgroup
\Gamma\subset\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q})
and any rational point
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})
, one has

(1)

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{\#\Gamma.x\cap B_{R}}{\omega_{\infty}(B_{R}\cap\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R}).x)}=\frac{\Vol(\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})/\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})\cap\Gamma)}{\Vol(\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})/\Gamma)}

where
\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}
denotes the stabilizer of
x
in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and volumes are induced from compatible choices of Haar measures on
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R}),\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})
and
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})/\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})
.
Then
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
is strongly Hardy–Littlewood with respect to
l
.

We remark that
if
\Gamma^{\prime}\subset\Gamma
is a finite-index subgroup, then the truth of Equa.(
1
) for
\Gamma^{\prime}
implies that for
\Gamma
.
Equa.(
1
) has been verified in many cases. For instance, when
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is maximal in a semisimple
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
without compact
\mathbb{Q}
-factors, all “algebraic height functions”
l
would work.
What is proved in this paper (see Section
6.8
) is that

Theorem 1.2
.

Assumptions same as above. There exists
l
such that Equa.(
1
) holds.
In particular,
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
is always strongly Hardy-Littlewood for some
l
.

Already when
\bm{\mathrm{G}}=\SL_{n}
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}=\SL_{n_{1}}\times...\times\SL_{n_{k}}
with
\sum n_{i}\leq n
, this seems unknown before.
Generalizations can be obtained by combining Theorem
6.42
with
[
WX16
]
.

1.2.
Manin conjecture for a pair

In the previous section, there is no emphasis on which height function
l
one should use.

In the context of integral points on projective varieties, roughly speaking, Manin conjecture makes prediction on the asymptotics of integral points with respect to heights associated to the anti-canonical bundle. In the case of a pair, one should replace the anti-canonical line bundle by the log anti-canonical line bundle.

1.2.1.
Log anti-canonical line bundles

Let
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
be a
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariant compactification of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}\cong\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{D}}:=\bm{\mathrm{X}}\setminus\bm{\mathrm{U}}
be the boundary.
We assume that
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
is smooth and
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
is a simple normal crossing divisor. Let
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}
be the canonical line bundle. Then
(\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}))^{\vee}
is called the
log anti-canonical
line bundle.

1.2.2.
Heights

Without loss of generality, we only consider the height at infinity here (see Remark
2.4
).
Let
\bm{\mathrm{D}}=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathscr{A}}\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}
be the decomposition of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
into absolutely irreducible components.
Let
\bm{\mathrm{L}}:=\sum\lambda_{\alpha}\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}
with
\lambda_{\alpha}>0
for all
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}
. View the line bundle
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{L}})
over
\mathbb{R}
in the category of smooth manifolds and equip it with a smooth metric
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
. Let
\bm{\mathrm{1}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}
be the canonical section of
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{L}})
and define for
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
,

\Ht(x):=\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{1}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}(x)\right\rVert^{-1}.

Points of height bounded by a fixed number is a compact subset of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
.

1.2.3.
Volume

Let
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime}:=\sum d_{\alpha}\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}
be a divisor supported on
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
. It is not necessary to require
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime}
to be effective or
d_{\alpha}\geq 0
, so
\bm{\mathrm{1}}_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime}}
is only a rational section that is regular on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
.
Let
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime}):=\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}\otimes\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime})
. Assume that
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime})
(again, viewed in the category of smooth manifolds) is equipped with a smooth metric
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
, then a
measure, denoted as
\Vol
, on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
can be locally defined by

(2)

\Vol=\frac{\left|\omega\right|}{\left\lVert\omega\otimes\bm{\mathrm{1}}_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime}}\right\rVert},\;\text{ for any nonvanishing local volume form }\omega.

1.2.4.
Haar measure

In the homogeneous setting, a natural choice of measure is the
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})
-invariant Haar measure.
For this we assume the existence of a nonzero
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-invariant top-degree differential form on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
. Let
\omega_{0}
be such a form and
\mathrm{m}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
be the measure on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
induced form it, then
\mathrm{m}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
is
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})
invariant.
Let
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime}:=-\divisor(\omega_{0})
, which we shall refer to as
the
anticanonical divisor, then
\bm{\mathrm{1}}_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime}}
is identified with
\omega_{0}^{\vee}
, the unique rational section of
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime})\cong\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}^{\vee}
(the dual of
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}
) such that the natural pairing between
\omega_{0}
and
\omega_{0}^{\vee}
is the constant function
1
.
Define a smooth metric on
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{\prime})
by imposing
\left\lVert(\omega_{0}\otimes\omega_{0}^{\vee})(x)\right\rVert=1
for every
x
.
Then the
\Vol
from Equa.(
2
) coincides with
\mathrm{m}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
here.

1.2.5.
The prediction

Fix an integral model
\mathcal{X}
of
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
and
\mathcal{D}
of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
. Then
\mathcal{U}:=\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{D}
is an integral model of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
. Or more generally, one can take
\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{Z}):=\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{\mathscr{K}}(\mathbb{Z})
as above.

Let
(D_{\alpha^{\prime}})_{\alpha^{\prime}\in\mathscr{A}^{\prime}}
be consisting of connected components of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{R})
as
\alpha
varies in
\mathscr{A}
. The analytic Clemens complex
\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R}}
, elements of which consist of connected components of nonempty intersection of
D_{\alpha^{\prime}}
’s, encodes the intersection patterns among boundary divisors.

Depending on the integral model, let
\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z}}
be the modified analytic Clemens complex consisting of faces
F\in\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R}}
with

\left(\bigcap_{\alpha\in F}D_{\alpha}\right)\bigcap\left(\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{U}({\mathbb{Z}})}\overline{\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}.x}\right)\neq\emptyset.

That is to say, we only consider closures of connected components of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
where there exists at least one integral point.
Let
E_{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{U}})
be the abelian group of invertible functions on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
modulo constant functions
\mathbb{Q}^{\times}
and

b:=\max\left\{\rk\Pic_{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{U}})-\rk E_{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{U}})+\#F\;\middle|\;F\text{ is a maximal face of }\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z}}\right\}.

When
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
is connected, e.g., when
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is semisimple and simply connected and
H^{1}(\mathbb{R},\bm{\mathrm{H}})
is trivial, the modified analytic Clemens complex coincide with the usual analytic Clemens complex. But in general, they could be different.

Here is a natural question inspired by Manin conjecture.

Question 1.3
.

Let
\Ht
be a height function associated with a log anti-canonical divisor.
Assume
\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{Z})\neq\emptyset
.
Does there exist a positive number
c>0
such that

\#\left\{x\in\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{Z})\;\middle|\;\Ht(x)\leq R\right\}\sim cR(\log R)^{b-1}

as
R\to+\infty
?

Here is what we can do on the positive side (combine Theorem
6.27
, Lemma
5.2
and Theorem
2.13
).

Theorem 1.4
.

Assume the following:

•

\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
are both connected, reductive and have no nontrivial
\mathbb{Q}
-characters;

•

the identity component of the centralizer of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is contained in
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
;

•

the projection of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
to the compact
\mathbb{Q}
-factor of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is surjective.

Then the log anti-canonical divisor is big and Question
1.3
admits a positive answer.

Note that under the assumptions made in the theorem,
\rk\Pic_{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{U}})=\rk E_{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{U}})=0
.

1.3.
Weighted counting

In this subsection, we assume that
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is semisimple without compact
\mathbb{Q}
-simple factors and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is reductive and connected. However, as opposed to previous discussions, we are mainly interested in the case when
\bm{\mathrm{H}}(\mathbb{R})\Gamma/\Gamma
has infinite volume.
In this case, the number of integral points of bounded height is no longer expected to be asymptotic to the volume. To remedy the situation, we consider weighted versions of the counting problem.

We also assume that
\bm{\mathrm{Z}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}})^{\circ}
is contained in
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
throughout this subsection.
First we discuss an analogue of Theorem
1.2
. Let
\omega_{\infty}
be an invariant measure on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
induced from some invariant form.

Theorem 1.5
.

There exists a height function
\Ht
(as in Section
1.2.2
) such that for every
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})
and every arithmetic lattice
\Gamma
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
, one has

\sum_{y\in\Gamma.x\cap B_{R,x}}\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{y}\sim\omega_{\infty}(B_{R,x})

where
B_{R,x}:=\left\{y\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}.x,\;\Ht(y)\leq R\right\}
.

See Theorem
6.26
.
We expect that for “most”
y\in\Gamma.x\cap B_{R,x}
,
\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{y}
grows like a power of
\log R
.

For a more general height, our result is much weaker.
Let
(\bm{\mathrm{X}},\bm{\mathrm{D}})
be a smooth
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-pair over
\mathbb{Q}
such that
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
is
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariantly isomorphic to
\bm{\mathrm{X}}\setminus\bm{\mathrm{D}}
.
Let
\Ht
be a height function associated with some divisor whose support is equal to
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
.
Let
\mathrm{G}:=\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}
,
\mathrm{H}_{x}:=\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathrm{G}
and assume that
\Gamma
is contained in
\mathrm{G}
.

Let

b^{\prime}:=\max\left\{\#F\;\middle|\;F\text{ is a maximal face of }\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R},\mathbb{Z}}\right\}.

The following will be proved in Theorem
6.27
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Let
\psi
be a non-negative compactly supported function whose support is large enough. For
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})
, define

\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{x}:=\sum_{h\in\mathrm{H}_{x}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}}\langle\psi,h_{*}\mathrm{m}_{[\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}]}\rangle^{-1}.

Then there exists
c>0
such that

\sum_{x\in\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{Z}),\,\Ht(x)\leq R}\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{x}\sim c\cdot R(\log R)^{b^{\prime}-1}.

1.4.
Examples

We provide three examples of different flavours. The first two examples are spherical varieties but the third one is not.

1.4.1.
Example I

Let
(Q_{1},\mathbb{Q}^{2})
and
(Q_{2},\mathbb{Q}^{4})
be two quadratic forms represented by symmetric matrices
M_{Q_{i}}
for
i=1,2
.
Let
\Mat_{4,2}(\mathbb{Z})
be the set of
4
-by-
2
matrices with integral coefficients and

\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{Z}):=\left\{M\in\Mat_{4,2}(\mathbb{Z})\;\middle|\;M^{\Tr}M_{Q_{2}}M=M_{Q_{1}}\right\}.

satisfying:

•

both
(Q_{1},\mathbb{R}^{2})
and
(Q_{2},\mathbb{R}^{4})
are split. And
(Q_{1},\mathbb{Q}^{2})
is
\mathbb{Q}
-anisotropic;

•

\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{Q})
is nonempty.

Hence we can find
(Q_{1}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q}^{2})
such that
(Q_{2},\mathbb{Q}^{4})
is isomorphic to
(Q_{1},\mathbb{Q}^{2})\oplus(Q_{1}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q}^{2})
. We further assume that

•

the orthogonal group of
(Q_{1},\mathbb{Q}^{2})
is not isomorphic to the orthogonal group of
(Q_{1}^{\prime},\mathbb{Q}^{2})
as linear algebraic groups over
\mathbb{Q}
.

Let
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
denote the Euclidean norm of a matrix.

Theorem 1.7
.

As
R
tends to infinity,
there exists some constant
c>0
such that

\#\left\{M\in\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{Z}),\;\left\lVert M\right\rVert\leq R\right\}\sim cR^{2}\log R.

See Theorem
7.1
for more details.

Remark 1.8
.

As suggested to us by Rudnick, such a result should be known, but we fail to identify a reference. Also, the many restrictions put here should be unnecessary. From the point view of the current paper, a discussion of the general case (especially, lifting the dimension restrictions on quadratic forms) seems possible if the explicit construction of resolution of singularities is understood. But this is too complicated to be discussed here.

1.4.2.
Example II

Let
\Lambda(n,1)
denote the set of splittings of
\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
as a direct sum of two subgroups, one of which has rank
n
. Each element
A\oplus B
of
\Lambda(n,1)
can be associated with
(v,M)
where
v\in\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
is an integral vector and
M
is an element in
\wedge^{n}\mathbb{Z}^{n+1}
where
A=\mathbb{Z}.v
,
M=v_{1}\wedge...\wedge v_{n}
with
B=\oplus_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{Z}.v_{i}
. Moreover,
v,M
are unique up to sign. Below
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert
denotes the natural Euclidean norm.

Theorem 1.9
.

For two positive integers
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}
, we have

\#\{[(v,M)]\in\Lambda(n,1),\;\left\lVert v\right\rVert^{\lambda_{1}}\left\lVert M\right\rVert^{\lambda_{2}}\leq R\}\sim\begin{cases}c_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\cdot R^{\frac{n}{\lambda_{1}}}\log(R),\quad&\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}\\
c_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}\cdot R^{\frac{n}{\min\{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\}}},\quad&\lambda_{1}\neq\lambda_{2}\end{cases}

for some
c_{\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}}>0
.

See Theorem
8.1
below for details.

1.4.3.
Example III

The third example concerns with the space of triangles.
Let
\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathbb{Z})
denote the set of three ordered linearly independent lines in
\mathbb{Q}^{3}
. For an element
(\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{1},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{2},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{3})\in\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathbb{Z})
, we take
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i}
to be a nonzero vector on
\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{i}
.
Here are two ways of measuring the “complexity” of such an element (of course, it is independent of the choice of
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i}
):

\displaystyle\Ht_{1}(\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{1},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{2},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{3}):=\frac{\prod_{i}\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i}\right\rVert^{2}}{\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{1}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{2}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{1}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{3}\right\rVert\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{2}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{3}\right\rVert}

\displaystyle\Ht_{2}(\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{1},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{2},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{3}):=\frac{\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{1}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{2}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{1}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{3}\right\rVert^{2}\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{2}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{3}\right\rVert^{2}}{(\prod_{i}\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{i}\right\rVert)\cdot\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{1}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{2}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{3}\right\rVert^{3}}.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}>0
and

\Ht(\bm{\mathrm{x}}):=\Ht_{1}(\bm{\mathrm{x}})^{\kappa_{1}}\Ht_{2}(\bm{\mathrm{x}})^{\kappa_{2}}
for
\bm{\mathrm{x}}\in\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}(\mathbb{R})
.
Then for some constant
c_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}>0
,

\sum_{\left\{\bm{\mathrm{x}}\in\mathcal{M}_{3}(\mathbb{Z})\;\middle|\;\Ht(x)\leq R\right\}}\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{x}\sim\begin{cases}c_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}\cdot R^{\frac{8}{3}\kappa_{1}^{-1}}\cdot\log(R)\quad&\kappa_{1}=\kappa_{2}\\
c_{\kappa_{1},\kappa_{2}}\cdot R^{\frac{8}{3}\max\{\kappa_{1}^{-1},\kappa_{2}^{-1}\}}\quad&\kappa_{1}\neq\kappa_{2}.\end{cases}

See Theorem
9.1
below for more details, especially the definition of
\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{x}
.

1.5.
Outline of the proof and future directions

1.5.1.
Outline

Here is an outline of the proof as well as this paper.

First, it is known that if
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
admits transitive action of a linear algebraic group
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
, then its set of integral points admits an action of some arithmetic subgroup
\Gamma
and furthermore, there are only finitely many orbits (see Theorem
2.3
).
Fix such an orbit
\Gamma.x
.
One wishes to compare the asymptotics of number of points of bounded height on
\Gamma.x
with volume of points of bounded height on
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}.x
.
The basics of height function are discussed in Section
2.1
.
We explain what “volume” is from the point view of invariant gauge forms and discuss its boundary behaviours in Section
2.2
. Particularly, Theorem
2.13
and
2.14
are important when one applies results from
[
CLT12
]
in later sections.

How to make this comparison?
It is discovered in
[
DRS93
]
that the comparison can be made if one solves certain equidistribution problem on
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}/\Gamma
.
The equidistribution problem alluded is about limiting behaviour of certain average of translates of a fixed homogeneous measure, which looks like:

\frac{1}{\Vol(B_{R,x})}\int_{[g]\in B_{R,x}}g_{*}\mathrm{m}_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})\Gamma/\Gamma}\,\Vol([g]).

Here
\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}
is the stabilizer of
x
in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
,
\Vol
refers to certain natural measure on
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})/\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})
and
B_{R,x}
are those cosets
g\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})
satisfying
\Ht(g.x)\leq R
. We elaborate on this in Section
3
.

The study of the limiting behaviors of the integrand in the above expression is the subject of
[
EMS96
]
and
[
Zha21
]
. Perhaps the most crucial input behind these works is the classification of unipotent-invariant ergodic probability measures due to Ratner
[
Ra91
]
.
We will review the relevant equidistribution and nondivergence results in Section
4
.

Theorems from
[
EMS96
,
Zha21
]

are most useful when
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is large in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
. When this is not the case, one may do the centralizer trick by considering, for instance,
\bm{\mathrm{G}}\times\bm{\mathrm{Z}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}\cdot\Delta(\bm{\mathrm{Z}})
where
\bm{\mathrm{Z}}
is contained in the centralizer of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.
This does not change the underlying variety, but by considering a larger automorphism group, we implicitly put restrictions on the heights under consideration.
The trick works better when
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is connected and reductive.

Anyway, assuming
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is large in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
in some sense, one can try to analyze the possible limiting measures of
(g_{*}\mathrm{m}_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})\Gamma/\Gamma})
as
g
varies.
The best thing is that for “most”
g
tending to infinity, the limit is the
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}
-invariant Haar measure on
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}/\Gamma
. When this fails, we say that
focusing
happens. The crucial thing is to understand whether focusing
1
1

1

We will rarely use the term focusing in the main body of the paper. The precise definition can be found in
[
EMS96
]
.
happens or not.

Preferably, focusing can be detected by finitely many vectors in finitely many linear representations.
If so, then we will manage to interpret these conditions in the language of algebraic varieties and their closed subvarieties and furthermore, by resolution of singularities for a pair, into language of smooth projective varieties and their simple normal crossing divisors.
Once this is done, the equidistribution theorem of
[
CLT12
]
becomes very useful.
We will discuss, in a rather abstract way, the relation between this and the counting problem in Section
5
.
Then in Section
6
, we will provide concrete group theoretical conditions under which abstract setup in Section
5
can be actually realized.
These will further be illustrated by three concrete examples in the last three sections.

1.5.2.
What remains to be done?

We list some questions that are related to the discussion of the current paper. Of course, one natural task is to verify the log Manin conjecture (which should correspond to the “non-focusing” case because of the “rigid” condition) recently proposed by
[
San23
]
. Here we want to be a bit more specific.

Let
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
be a homogeneous variety over
\mathbb{Q}
isomorphic to
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
. Assume
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})\neq\emptyset
. For
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})
, we let
\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}
be the stabilizer of
x
in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.
Let
\mathrm{G}:=\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}
,
\mathrm{H}_{x}:=\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}(\mathbb{R})\cap\mathrm{G}
and
\Gamma
be an arithmetic subgroup contained in
\mathrm{G}
.
Let
\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{H}_{x}]}
,
\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{G}]}
and
\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{H}_{x}}
denote invariant measures (so we need to assume that they exist) on
\mathrm{H}_{x}\Gamma/\Gamma
,
\mathrm{G}/\Gamma
and
\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{H}_{x}
respectively. We require these measures to be compatible. Namely, if
\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{H}_{x}}
and
\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{G}}
are the corresponding invariant measures on
\mathrm{H}_{x}
and
\mathrm{G}
respectively that induce

\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{H}_{x}]}
and
\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{G}]}
, then for every compactly supported function
f
on
\mathrm{G}
, one has

\int\left(\int f(gh)\,\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{H}_{x}}(h)\right)\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{H}_{x}}([g])=\int f(x)\,\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{G}}(x).

1.5.3.
Finite-volume case

Assume

•

\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
are both connected;

•

all the nontrivial
\mathbb{Q}
-factors of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
are noncompact over
\mathbb{R}
;

•

\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
have no nontrivial
\mathbb{Q}
-characters. Equivalently,
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})/\Gamma
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}(\mathbb{R})\Gamma/\Gamma
have finite volume.

We expect that there exists a class of good heights (or, good boundary divisors) such that

(3)

\#\left\{y\in\Gamma.x\;\middle|\;\Ht(y)\leq R\right\}\sim\frac{\left\lvert\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{H}_{x}]}\right\rvert}{\left\lvert\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{G}]}\right\rvert}\cdot\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{H}_{x}}\left(\left\{y\in\mathrm{G}.x\;\middle|\;\Ht(y)\leq R\right\}\right)

for every
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})
and every arithmetic lattice
\Gamma
(of course, it is sufficient to verify this for some finite-index subgroup of
\Gamma
).

Question 1.11
.

Assume that
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is large in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and all the invariant measures exist.
Let
(\bm{\mathrm{X}},\bm{\mathrm{D}})
be a smooth
\mathrm{G}
-pair over
\mathbb{Q}
with
\bm{\mathrm{X}}\setminus\bm{\mathrm{D}}
equivariantly isomorphic to
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
.
Is Equa.(
3
) true for the log anti-canonical height on
(\bm{\mathrm{X}},\bm{\mathrm{D}})
?

Being large does not have a precise definition. For instance, one may say that
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is large in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
if one of the following is true:

1.

\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is semisimple and
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is a spherical variety;

2.

\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
are reductive and the identity component of the centralizer of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is contained in
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
.

1.5.4.
Infinite-volume case

Assume that

•

\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is connected, semisimple and all
\mathbb{Q}
-simple factors are noncompact;

•

\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is connected, reductive and has nontrivial
\mathbb{Q}
-characters.

In this case, we no longer expect the asymptotic of integral points is the same as the volume asymptotic on
\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{H}
. However, we do have a replacement of the volume asymptotic.

Recall
\mathscr{P}^{\max}_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
is the collection of maximal parabolic
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroups containing
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
. For each
\bm{\mathrm{P}}\in\mathscr{P}^{\max}_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
, we fix an integral vector
2
2

2

Implicitly, an integral structure
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{Z}}
of
\mathfrak{g}
is fixed.

\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}}\in\wedge^{\dim\bm{\mathrm{P}}}\mathfrak{g}
that lifts the Lie algebra of
\bm{\mathrm{P}}
.
Let
\Ht
be a height function on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
and let
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})
.
Let
\bm{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime}_{x}
be the largest connected
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}
where all the
\mathbb{Q}
-characters of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}
vanish.
For
\eta,R>0
, define the following region

B_{\eta,R,x}:=\left\{[g]\in\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{H}_{x}^{\prime}\;\middle|\;\Ht(g.x)\leq R,\;\left\lVert\Ad(g)\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}}\right\rVert\geq\eta,\;\forall\,\bm{\mathrm{P}}\in\mathscr{P}^{\max}_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}}\right\}.

In favorable situations, we expect that the asymptotic of
\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{x}}\left(B_{\eta,R,x}\right)
as
R\to+\infty
is independent of
\eta
.

Question 1.12
.

Fix
\eta>0
.
For what height functions does

\#\left\{y\in\Gamma.x\;\middle|\;\Ht(y)\leq R\right\}\sim\frac{1}{\left\lvert\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{G}]}\right\rvert}\cdot\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{x}}\left(B_{\eta,R,x}\right)\;\text{ as }R\to+\infty

hold for every
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})
and every arithmetic subgroup
\Gamma
?

Question 1.13
.

Given
\eta>0
, what is the asymptotic of
\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{x}}\left(B_{\eta,R,x}\right)
?

In the case of Example III (Section
1.4.3
) discussed above, it seems that
\mathrm{m}_{\mathrm{G}/\mathrm{H}^{\prime}_{x}}\left(B_{\eta,R,x}\right)
can be expressed in terms of the norms of sections of certain line bundles.

Notations

For a linear algebraic group
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
over a field
k
, let the gothic letter
\mathfrak{g}
denote its Lie algebra over
k
.
When the notation looks cumbersome, we also use
\Lie(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
.
Let
\mathfrak{X}^{*}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
(resp.
\mathfrak{X}^{*}_{k}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
) be the group of characters (resp.
k
-characters) of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.
Similarly
\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{k}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
is the group of cocharacters over
k
.
Following
[
BS73
]
, when
k=\mathbb{Q}
, we let

{}^{\circ}\bm{\mathrm{G}}:=\bigcap_{\alpha\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})}\ker\alpha^{2}.

Let
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}
be the quotient
\mathbb{Q}
-split torus
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/{}^{\circ}\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and

p^{\spl}
be the natural quotient morphism
\bm{\mathrm{G}}\to\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}
.

Assume
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
to be connected and
let
\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
be its unipotent radical,
then
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is equal to

\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\red}\ltimes\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})=(\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\semisimple}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}}))\ltimes\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})=\left(\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\cpt}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\nc}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})^{\an}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})^{\spl}\right)\ltimes\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})

where
\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\red}
is a connected reductive
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup (called a Levi subgroup) lifting
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
,
\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\semisimple}=[\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\red},\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\red}]
is a connected semisimple
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup,
\bm{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
is the identity component of the center of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\red}
(called the central torus),
\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\cpt}
is the product of
\mathbb{Q}
-simple factors of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\semisimple}
that are
\mathbb{R}
-anisotropic,
\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\nc}

is the product of
\mathbb{Q}
-simple factors of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\semisimple}
that are
\mathbb{R}
-isotropic.
A
\mathbb{Q}
-torus
\bm{\mathrm{T}}
can be written uniquely as an almost direct product of
\bm{\mathrm{T}}^{\an}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{T}}^{\spl}
, a
\mathbb{Q}
-anisotropic subtorus and a
\mathbb{Q}
-split subtorus. This explains the notation
\bm{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})^{\an}
and
\bm{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})^{\spl}
above.

Let
\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}
denote the topological adelic ring of the field of rational numbers
\mathbb{Q}
.
\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}}
denotes the Galois group of the extension
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}/\mathbb{Q}
.

We say that
(\bm{\mathrm{X}},\bm{\mathrm{D}})
is a
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-pair over
\mathbb{Q}
if
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
is a projective variety over
\mathbb{Q}
,
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
is a closed subvariety over
\mathbb{Q}
and
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
is equipped with the action of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
such that
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
is preserved under this action.
A pair
(\bm{\mathrm{X}},\bm{\mathrm{D}})
is said to be a smooth pair over
\mathbb{Q}
if
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
is a smooth projective variety over
\mathbb{Q}
and
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
is a simple/strict normal crossing divisor over
\mathbb{Q}
.

For locally finite measures
(\nu_{n})
and
\nu
on a locally compact second countable Hausdorff space
X
, following
[
SZ19
]
, we say that
[\nu_{n}]
converges to
[\nu]
and write
\lim_{n\to\infty}[\nu_{n}]=[\nu]
if there exists
(a_{n})\subset\mathbb{R}_{>0}
such that
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}\nu_{n}=\nu
under the weak-
*
topology.

2.
Arithmetic and Geometry of Equivariant Compactifications

For this section we further assume the following notations:

•

\bm{\mathrm{X}}
is a smooth projective variety over
\mathbb{Q}
of dimension
d
;

•

\bm{\mathrm{D}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{X}}
is a simple normal crossing divisor over
\mathbb{Q}
and
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
is the complement of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
in
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
;

•

(\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha})_{\alpha\in\mathscr{A}}
are the irreducible components of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
over the algebraic closure
\overline{\mathbb{Q}}
. This set and its index set
\mathscr{A}
are equipped with an action of
\Gal_{\mathbb{Q}}
;

•

\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is a linear algebraic group over
\mathbb{Q}
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.

2.1.
Integral points, adelic heights and orbits of arithmetic groups

2.1.1.
Integral points

Let
\Val
be the set of valuations on
\mathbb{Q}
up to equivalence, identified with the set of prime numbers and the Archimedean place
\{\infty\}
. By convention
\mathbb{Q}_{\infty}:=\mathbb{R}
.
Let
\Val_{f}
denote only the set of finite primes.
For a finite set
S\subset\Val_{f}
, let
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
denote the set of rational numbers that are integral outside
S
.

Consider the space of adelic points given by certain restricted product

\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}}):={\prod}^{\prime}_{p\in\Val}\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\times\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})

equipped with adelic topology (see
[
Con12
]
for details).
Let
\mathscr{K}_{f}
be a nonempty open compact subset of
\prod_{p\in\Val_{f}}\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
. More concretely, there exists a finite set
S\subset\Val_{f}
and a smooth model
(\mathcal{X},\mathcal{D})
of
(\bm{\mathrm{X}},\bm{\mathrm{D}})
over
\Spec\mathbb{Z}_{S}
such that

\mathscr{K}_{f}=K_{S}\times\prod_{p\notin S}K_{p}
for some open compact subset
K_{S}\subset\prod_{p\in S}\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and

(4)

K_{p}=(\mathcal{X}\setminus\mathcal{D})(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\quad\forall\,p\notin S.

For
\mathscr{K}=\mathscr{K}_{f}\times K_{\infty}
where
K_{\infty}
is a union of some connected components of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
, define

\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{\mathscr{K}}(\mathbb{Z}):=\bm{\mathrm{X}}(\mathbb{Q})\bigcap\mathscr{K}_{f}\times K_{\infty}.

This adelic point of view contains the classical case of taking the integral points of some integral model as a special case.

2.1.2.
Adelic heights

As a reference, see
[
CLT10
]
.

Let
(L,s)
be a line bundle over
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
together with a global section
s\in\Gamma(\bm{\mathrm{X}},L)
.
An
adelic metric

\left(\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p}\right)_{p\in\Val}
is the following:

1.

for each
p\in\Val
, the
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p}
is a smooth metric on the analytic line bundle
L(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
over the analytic manifold
\bm{\mathrm{X}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
;

2.

there exist a finite set
S\subset\Val_{f}
and a smooth model
\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{X}
over
\Spec\mathbb{Z}_{S}
such that
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p}
is defined by

(5)

\left\lVert s(x)\right\rVert_{p}\leq 1\iff s(x)\in\widetilde{x}^{*}\mathcal{L}

where
x\in\bm{\mathrm{X}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and
\widetilde{x}\in\mathcal{X}(\mathbb{Z}_{p})
is a lift of
x
.

By saying that a metric
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p}
is
smooth
, we mean that for every nonzero local section
s
, the map
x\mapsto\left\lVert s(x)\right\rVert_{p}
is a smooth function on the locus where
s
does not vanish.
For simplicity,
L
together with this adelic metric is called an
adelic line bundle
.

If
L=\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{L}})
for some effective divisor
\bm{\mathrm{L}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}
is the canonical section, then for some finite set
S
,

(6)

\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}(x)\right\rVert_{p}=1,\quad\forall\,p\notin S,\;x\in K_{p}.

Indeed, since
\bm{\mathrm{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}
is a local generator of
L
over
\mathbb{Q}
, it is also a local generator of
\mathcal{L}
over
\bm{\mathrm{Z}}_{S}
for some finite
S
.
Take
S
larger such that Equa.(
5
) also holds.
Then Equa.(
6
) follows from Equa.(
5
) above.

Assume that the support of
\bm{\mathrm{L}}
is contained in
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
. We define an
adelic height function
on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})
by

\Ht\left((x_{p})\right):=\left(\prod_{p\in\Val}\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}(x_{p})\right\rVert_{p}\right)^{-1}

Now we choose a finite
S\subset\Val_{f}
large enough such that Equa.(
4
,
5
,
6
) hold and moreover, the integral models
\mathcal{X}
appearing in Equa.(
4
) and (
5
) coincide over
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
.
Then

\Ht\left((x_{p})\right)=\left(\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}(x_{\infty})\right\rVert_{\infty}\cdot\prod_{p\in S}\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}(x_{p})\right\rVert_{p}\right)^{-1},\;\forall\,(x_{p})\in\mathscr{K}.

A great advantage of adelic height is that, by the product formula, this height is independent of the choice of sections.

2.1.3.
Orbits of arithmetic subgroups

Let
S\subset\Val_{f}
,
\mathscr{K}
and an adelic line bundle
(L,(\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p})_{p\in\Val})
be the same as last subsection.
Assume that
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
is a
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariant compactification of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
is the complement of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
.
By enlarging
S
, we assume further that we have a smooth model
\mathcal{G}
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
and that the action
\bm{\mathrm{G}}\times\bm{\mathrm{U}}\to\bm{\mathrm{U}}
over
\mathbb{Q}
extends to
\mathcal{G}\times\mathcal{U}\to\mathcal{U}
over
\mathbb{Z}_{S}
.
Note the following:

Lemma 2.1
.

For
p\in\Val_{f}
, the subgroup

\Stab_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p}):=\left\{g\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})\;\middle|\;\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}\right\rVert_{g.x}=\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}\right\rVert_{x},\;\forall\,x\in K_{p}\right\}

contains an open and compact subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
.

Proof.

Indeed, the function
x\mapsto\left\lVert s_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}(x)\right\rVert_{p}
is smooth and hence locally constant on
\mathcal{U}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
and in particular on
K_{p}
. By compactness and the totally disconnectedness of p-adic topology, we can cover
K_{p}
by disjoint open sets such that
\left\lVert s_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}(x)\right\rVert_{p}
is constant on each open set. By choosing a small enough open compact subgroup
K\subset\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q}_{p})
, we can guarantee that each open subset is
K
-stable. Such a
K
is thus contained in
\Stab_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p})
, proving the claim.
∎

Let

\Gamma_{p}:=\begin{cases}\text{an open, compact subgroup of }\Stab_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{p}),\quad p\in S\\
\mathcal{G}(\mathbb{Z}_{p}),\quad p\notin S,\end{cases}

and

\Gamma:=\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q})\cap\prod_{p\in\Val_{f}}\Gamma_{p}.

Discussions above prove the following:

Lemma 2.2
.

The
\Gamma
defined above is an arithmetic subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q})
that preserves
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{\mathscr{K}}(\mathbb{Z})
. Moreover,

\Ht_{p}(x)=\Ht_{p}(\gamma.x),\;\forall\,p\in\Val_{f},\;x=(x_{p})\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{\mathscr{K}}(\mathbb{Z}),\;\gamma\in\Gamma

where
\Ht_{p}(x):=\left\lVert\bm{\mathrm{s}}_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}(x_{p})\right\rVert_{p}^{-1}
.

The theorem below follows from
[
GMB13
, Proposition 7.13, 7.14]
. Special cases can be proved using reduction theory (see
[
Bor19
, Remark 6.4]
).

Theorem 2.3
.

There are only finitely many
\Gamma
-orbits on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{\mathscr{K}}(\mathbb{Z})
.

We sketch a proof following
[
WX16
]
assuming
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is connected for the sake of completeness.

Sketch of proof assuming
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
to be connected.

The conclusion is going to follow from a spreading-out argument, finiteness of Shafarevich–Tate groups and finiteness of class numbers of linear algebraic groups.
We define

x\sim_{\loc}y\iff\Gamma_{p}.x=\Gamma_{p}.y,\;\forall p\in\Val_{f}

for two elements
x=(x_{p})
and
y=(y_{p})
in
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{\mathscr{K}}(\mathbb{Z})
. Let
[x]_{\loc}
denote the equivalence class containing
x
.
As a first step, we note that
\{[x]_{\loc}\}
is finite (compare
[
BR95
, Lemma 1.6.4]
).
Indeed, for
p\notin S
, there always exists
\gamma_{p}\in\Gamma_{p}
such that
y_{p}=\gamma_{p}x_{p}
by Hensel’s lemma and Lang’s theorem on triviality of the Galois cohomology group
H^{1}(F,\bm{\mathrm{H}})
for a finite field
F
and connected
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
. So it remains to show that for each
p\in S

,
K_{p}\cap\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})
is finite modulo the equivalence relation defined by
x\sim y
iff
\Gamma_{p}.x=\Gamma_{p}.y
, which follows from the finiteness of
K_{p}/\Gamma_{p}
.

We define an equivalence relation
\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}
on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{\mathscr{K}}(\mathbb{Z})
by

x\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}y\iff\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q}).x=\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q}).y.

Then we show that for each
x
, the number of equivalence classes
[x]_{\loc}/\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}
is finite.
Indeed, by inspecting the following commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{H}}(\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\bm{\mathrm{H}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{H}}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\prod_{p\in\Val_{f}}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\bm{\mathrm{H}}),}

we have an injection

[x]_{\loc}/\sim_{\mathbb{Q}}\hookrightarrow\ker\left(H^{1}(\mathbb{Q},\bm{\mathrm{H}})\to\prod_{p}H^{1}(\mathbb{Q}_{p},\bm{\mathrm{H}})\right).

But the latter is finite (See
[
BS64
, Theorem 7.1]
).

For
y\in[x]_{\loc}
, let
[y]_{\loc,\mathbb{Q}}:=[x]_{\loc}\cap[y]_{\mathbb{Q}}
denote the equivalence class containing
y
. As the final step, we show that
\Gamma\backslash[y]_{\loc,\mathbb{Q}}
is finite for every
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{\mathscr{K}}(\mathbb{Z})
and
y\in[x]_{\loc}
. For each
z\in[y]_{\loc,\mathbb{Q}}
, we find
q_{z}\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q})
and
\gamma_{z}\in\prod\Gamma_{p}
such that
z=q_{z}y=\gamma_{z}y
. Then the map

\displaystyle\Gamma\backslash\left[y\right]_{\loc,\mathbb{Q}}

\displaystyle\to\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{y}(\mathbb{Q})\backslash\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{y}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})/\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{y}(\mathbb{A}_{\mathbb{Q}})\cap\prod\Gamma_{p}

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\mapsto q_{z}^{-1}\gamma_{z}

is well-defined and injective. As the right hand side is finite (see
[
PRR23
, Theorem 5.8]
or
[
Bor63
, Theorem 5.1]
), we are done.

∎

Remark 2.4
.

In light of Lemma
2.2
and Theorem
2.3
, it makes no difference to count with respect to the adelic height or the height at infinity.

2.2.
Invariant gauge forms

Assume that
(\bm{\mathrm{X}},\bm{\mathrm{D}})
is a smooth
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-pair over
\mathbb{Q}
with
\bm{\mathrm{U}}=\bm{\mathrm{X}}\setminus\bm{\mathrm{D}}
isomorphic to
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}

\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariantly. Let
o\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q})
denote the identity coset.
Let
\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}
be the tangent bundle of
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
,
\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}^{*}
be its dual and
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}:=\det(\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}^{*})
be the canonical line bundle.
Let
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
denote the natural pairing between
\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}
and
\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}^{*}
and their wedge products.

An element
g\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{C})
acts on
\bm{\mathrm{X}}(\mathbb{C})
and hence on tangent vectors by
(g,v)\mapsto g_{*}v
and differential forms by
(g,\omega)\mapsto g^{*}\omega
. From the definition, one has that for every analytically open subset
\mathcal{O}\subset\bm{\mathrm{X}}(\mathbb{C})
,
\omega\in\Gamma(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{T}^{*}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}})
and
\partial\in\Gamma(\mathcal{O},\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}})
,

\langle\omega,\partial\rangle_{g.x}=\langle g^{*}\omega,(g^{-1})_{*}\partial\rangle_{x},\;\forall\,x\in g^{-1}\mathcal{O},\;g\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{C}).

Identify the fibre
\mathcal{T}_{o}(\bm{\mathrm{X}})
(resp.
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{o}(\bm{\mathrm{X}})
) of the tangent (resp. cotangent) bundle at
o
with
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
(resp.
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})^{*}
). The natural pairing on

((\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})^{*},\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})
is then identified with that on
(\mathcal{T}^{*}_{o}(\bm{\mathrm{X}}),\mathcal{T}_{o}(\bm{\mathrm{X}}))
. Let
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}
(resp.
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
) denote the determinant character of the adjoint action of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
(resp.
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
) on
\mathfrak{g}
(resp.
\mathfrak{h}
), that is,
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(g)=\det(\Ad(g),\mathfrak{g})
and
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(h)=\det(\Ad(h),\mathfrak{h})
.

Since
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
stabilizes
o
, its actions on
\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}
and
\mathcal{T}^{*}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}
induce actions on
\mathcal{T}_{o}(\bm{\mathrm{X}})
and
\mathcal{T}_{o}^{*}(\bm{\mathrm{X}})
. These actions are identified with the adjoint action of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
on
\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h}
and
(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})^{*}
respectively.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\det(h_{*},\mathcal{T}_{o}(\bm{\mathrm{X}}))=\det(\Ad(h),\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})=\frac{\det(\Ad(h),\mathfrak{g})}{\det(\Ad(h),\mathfrak{h})}=\frac{\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(h)}{\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(h)}.

and

\det((h^{-1})^{*},\mathcal{T}^{*}_{o}(\bm{\mathrm{X}}))=\det(h_{*},\mathcal{T}_{o}(\bm{\mathrm{X}}))^{-1}=\frac{\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(h)}{\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(h)}.

Define
(\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}})^{-1}:=\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}^{-1}\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
, an element of
\mathfrak{X}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}})
.

For a
\mathbb{Q}
-character
\alpha
of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
, let
\mathcal{L}_{\alpha}
be the GIT quotient of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}\times\Spec\mathbb{Q}[x]
by the right action of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
defined by

(g,\lambda)\cdot h:=(gh,\alpha(h)^{-1}\lambda).

It is a
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-linearized line bundle over
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
. On the other hand, given a
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-linearized line bundle
\mathcal{L}
over
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
, the (left) action of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
on the fibre of
o
gives a
\mathbb{Q}
-character
\alpha_{\mathcal{L}}
of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
. One can check the following:

Lemma 2.5
.

Let
\mathcal{L}
be a
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-linearized line bundle, then
\mathcal{L}\cong\mathcal{L}_{\alpha_{\mathcal{L}}}
. In particular,
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}\cong\mathcal{L}_{\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{-1}}
.

Lemma 2.6
.

Let
\chi
be a
\mathbb{Q}
-character of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
,

\Gamma(\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}},\mathcal{L}_{\chi})\cong\left\{f\in\mathbb{Q}[\bm{\mathrm{G}}]\;\middle|\;f(gh)=\chi^{-1}(h)f(g),\;\forall(g,h)\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}\times\bm{\mathrm{H}}\right\}.

By
[
KKV89
, 2.2, 2.3]
, the nonvanishing global sections of
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
consist of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-eigenvectors. Combined with the lemma above, we have

Lemma 2.7
.

The nonvanishing global sections of
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
consist of

\Gamma(\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}},\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}})^{\times}\cong\left\{f\in\mathbb{Q}[\bm{\mathrm{G}}]\;\middle|\;f=\lambda\chi,\;\exists\,\lambda\in\mathbb{Q}^{\times},\;\chi\in\mathfrak{X}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{*}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})\text{ with }\chi|_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}=\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}\right\}.

Hence we may label, up to a scalar, nonvanishing global sections of
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
as
\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi}
with
\chi
ranging over extensions of
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
to
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.
In particular,
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
is trivial iff
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
extends to a
\mathbb{Q}
-character on
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.

If both
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
and
\chi
are trivial characters, we abbreviate
\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}:=\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi}
and call it an
invariant gauge form
.
One may wish to compare the discussions above with those in

[
Rag72
, Chapter 1]
or
[
Wei82
, Chapter 2]
.

Lemma 2.8
.

If
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
is trivial, then
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is observable in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
2.7
,
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{-1}
extends to a character
\beta
on
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and take some nonzero vector
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\beta}
in the one dimensional representation where
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
acts by
\beta
.
Let
\bm{\mathrm{N}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}})^{\beta}
be the stabilizer of
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\beta}
, where
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\in\wedge^{\dim\bm{\mathrm{H}}}\mathfrak{g}
is a lift of
\mathfrak{h}
.

Then
\bm{\mathrm{N}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}})^{\beta}
is observable in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
by definition.
On the other hand,
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is contained in
\bm{\mathrm{N}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}})^{\beta}
as a normal subgroup and is hence observable in
\bm{\mathrm{N}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}})^{\beta}
. By the transitivity of observability,
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is observable in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

The converse is not true. For instance, if
\bm{\mathrm{G}}:=\SL_{3}
and

\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is generated by elements of the form

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}t&&\\
&t&\\
&&t^{-2}\end{array}\right]\cdot\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&&\\
&1&s\\
&&1\end{array}\right].

Then
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is observable in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
yet
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
is not extendable to
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.

2.3.
Boundary components of anti-canonical divisors

Keep assumptions in last subsection and assume that
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
is a trivial line bundle.
Here we are interested in the anti-canonical divisor
-\divisor(\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi})
on
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
where
\chi\in\mathfrak{X}^{*}_{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
is a fixed extension of
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
.

Note that
\mathfrak{g}
naturally maps to
\Gamma(\bm{\mathrm{X}},\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}})
, inducing

\wedge^{d}\mathfrak{g}\to\Gamma(\bm{\mathrm{X}},\wedge^{d}\mathcal{T}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}})\cong\Gamma(\bm{\mathrm{X}},(\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}})^{*}).

For
\bm{\mathrm{v}}=v_{1}\wedge...\wedge v_{d}\in\wedge^{d}\mathfrak{g}
, let
\partial_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}
denote its image.
Then for each
\bm{\mathrm{v}}\in\wedge^{d}\mathfrak{g}
,

\varphi_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}(x):=\langle\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi},\partial_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}\rangle_{x}

defines a rational function on
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
that is regular on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
.
Thus, to understand the boundary behaviour of
\omega^{\chi}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
, it suffices to understand that of
\varphi_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}
and
\partial_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}
.

Take a nonzero element
\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\in\wedge^{d}(\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{h})^{*}\hookrightarrow\wedge^{d}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
.
So
\left(\omega^{\chi}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}\right)_{o}=c_{\chi}\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathfrak{h}}
for some
c_{\chi}\neq 0
.

Lemma 2.10
.

For
g\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{C})
,

\varphi_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}(g.o)=c_{\chi}\chi(g)\cdot\langle\,\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathfrak{h}},\Ad(g)^{-1}\bm{\mathrm{v}}\,\rangle
.

Proof.

This follows from a direct computation:

\displaystyle\langle\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi},\partial_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}\rangle_{g.o}

\displaystyle=\chi(g)\cdot\langle(g^{-1})^{*}\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi},\partial_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}\rangle_{g.o}

\displaystyle=\chi(g)\cdot\langle\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi},(g^{-1})_{*}\partial_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}\rangle_{o}

\displaystyle=\chi(g)\cdot\langle\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi},\partial_{\Ad(g)^{-1}\bm{\mathrm{v}}}\rangle_{o}

\displaystyle=c_{\chi}\chi(g)\cdot\langle\,\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathfrak{h}},\Ad(g)^{-1}\bm{\mathrm{v}}\rangle.

∎

2.3.1.
Dual

To simplify the conclusion of Lemma
2.10
, we use a dual operation.

Fix
\omega_{0}\in\wedge^{\dim\bm{\mathrm{G}}}\mathfrak{g}^{*}
and
\omega_{0}^{*}\in\wedge^{\dim\bm{\mathrm{G}}}\mathfrak{g}
with
\langle\omega_{0}^{*},\omega_{0}\rangle=1
.
For two positive integers
l+l^{\prime}=\dim\bm{\mathrm{G}}
, we have a linear isomorphism:

\displaystyle\wedge^{l}\mathfrak{g}

\displaystyle\to\wedge^{l^{\prime}}\mathfrak{g}^{*}\cong(\wedge^{l^{\prime}}\mathfrak{g})^{*}

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{v}}

\displaystyle\mapsto\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\vee}

defined by

\bm{\mathrm{v}}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{w}}=\langle\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\vee},\bm{\mathrm{w}}\rangle\omega_{0}^{*},\;\forall\,\bm{\mathrm{w}}\in\wedge^{l^{\prime}}\mathfrak{g}.

Note that
\langle\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\vee},\bm{\mathrm{w}}\rangle=\langle\bm{\mathrm{v}},\bm{\mathrm{w}}^{\vee}\rangle
.
Furthermore,

\displaystyle\langle(\Ad(g)\bm{\mathrm{v}})^{\vee},\bm{\mathrm{w}}\rangle\omega_{0}^{*}

\displaystyle=\Ad(g)\bm{\mathrm{v}}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{w}}=\Ad(g)(\bm{\mathrm{v}}\wedge\Ad(g)^{-1}\bm{\mathrm{w}})

\displaystyle=\Ad(g)\left(\langle\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\vee},\Ad(g^{-1})\bm{\mathrm{w}}\rangle\omega_{0}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(g)\langle\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\vee},\Ad(g^{-1})\bm{\mathrm{w}}\rangle\omega_{0}^{*}.

Thus,
(\Ad(g)\bm{\mathrm{v}})^{\vee}=\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(g)\Ad^{*}(g)\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\vee}
.
So we see that
(\cdot)^{\vee}
intertwines
\Ad
and
\Ad^{*}\otimes\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}
, or equivalently,
\Ad\otimes\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}^{-1}
and
\Ad^{*}
.

Now take a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}
lifting
\mathfrak{h}
such that
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}^{\vee}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathfrak{h}}
.
Then we have

\displaystyle\langle\,\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{\mathfrak{h}},\Ad(g)^{-1}\bm{\mathrm{v}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Ad^{*}(g)\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\vee}_{\mathfrak{h}},\bm{\mathrm{v}}\rangle=\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(g)^{-1}\langle\left(\Ad(g)\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\right)^{\vee},\bm{\mathrm{v}}\rangle

\displaystyle=\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(g)^{-1}\langle\Ad(g)\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}},\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\vee}\rangle.

Combined with Lemma
2.10
above, we have

Lemma 2.11
.

For
g\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{C})
,

\varphi_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}{(g.o)}=c_{\chi}\chi(g)\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(g)^{-1}\langle\Ad(g)\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}},\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\vee}\rangle.

2.3.2.
The conclusion

For
\bm{\mathrm{v}}\in\wedge^{d}\mathfrak{g}
and
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}
,
we define integers
d_{\alpha}
,
d_{\alpha}^{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}
and
d_{\alpha}^{\varphi,\bm{\mathrm{v}}}
by

\displaystyle-\divisor(\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi}):=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathscr{A}}d_{\alpha}\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha},\;\divisor(\partial_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}):=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathscr{A}}d^{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}_{\alpha}\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha},\;-\divisor(\varphi_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}):=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathscr{A}}d^{\varphi,\bm{\mathrm{v}}}_{\alpha}\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}.

By the definition of
\varphi_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}
, we have

(7)

-\divisor(\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi})=\divisor\partial_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}-\divisor(\varphi_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}),\;\text{or }\;d_{\alpha}=d^{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}_{\alpha}+d^{\varphi,\bm{\mathrm{v}}}_{\alpha},\;\forall\,\alpha\in\mathscr{A}.

Each
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}
has dimension strictly smaller than
d
, one sees that
d^{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}_{\alpha}\geq 1
for every
\bm{\mathrm{v}}
and
\alpha
.
As a corollary of Lemma
2.11
we have

Theorem 2.12
.

Let
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}
,
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{C})
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}
be the stabilizer of
x
in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
. Let
(g_{n})
be a sequence in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{C})
such that

(1)

\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{n}.x\in\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{C})^{\circ}
and
\left\lvert\chi(g_{n})\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(g_{n})^{-1}\right\rvert=1
;

(2)

\left(\Ad(g_{n}).\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}_{x}}\right)
is bounded away from
\bm{\mathrm{0}}
.

Then
d_{\alpha}\geq 1
. If additionally

(3)

\left(\Ad(g_{n}).\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}_{x}}\right)
diverges to
\infty
,

then
d_{\alpha}\geq 2
.

Here
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{C})^{\circ}
denotes the points in
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{C})
that do not lie in any other
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\beta}(\mathbb{C})
.

Proof.

Without loss of generality assume
x=o
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}=\bm{\mathrm{H}}
.
Note that after passing to a subsequence,

\displaystyle\left(\Ad(g_{n}).\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\right)\text{ bounded away from }\bm{\mathrm{0}}

\displaystyle\implies l\left(\Ad(g_{n}).\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\right)>c,\,\exists\,l\in(\wedge^{d}\mathfrak{g})^{*},\,c>0;

\displaystyle\left(\Ad(g_{n}).\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\right)\text{ diverges }

\displaystyle\implies l\left(\Ad(g_{n}).\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\right)\text{ diverges},\,\exists\,l\in(\wedge^{d}\mathfrak{g})^{*}.

By Lemma
2.11
and our assumption, the first line implies the existence of
\bm{\mathrm{v}}\in\wedge^{d}\mathfrak{g}
such that
d_{\alpha}^{\varphi,\bm{\mathrm{v}}}\geq 0
, implying
d_{\alpha}\geq 1+d_{\alpha}^{\varphi,\bm{\mathrm{v}}}\geq 1
. Similarly, the second line implies
d_{\alpha}\geq 2
.
∎

There are two special cases that we would like to mention.

Theorem 2.13
.

Assume that
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is reductive and
\chi\cdot\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}^{-1}
is trivial. Then
d_{\alpha}\geq 1
for every
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}
. If additionally,
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is reductive and
\bm{\mathrm{Z}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}}^{\circ})^{\circ}\subset\bm{\mathrm{H}}
, then
d_{\alpha}\geq 2
for every
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}
.

Note that when
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is reductive,
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
is trivial and hence
\mathcal{K}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
is trivial. And if
\chi
corresponds to the trivial extension of
\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
, then
\chi\cdot\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}^{-1}
is trivial.

Proof.

If
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is also reductive, then
\bm{\mathrm{G}}.\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}
is closed.
Indeed, for
\bm{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime}:=\bm{\mathrm{N}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}})^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}
, the stabilizer of
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}
, the identity component of its centralizer in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is in
\bm{\mathrm{H}}^{\prime}
, thus the closedness follows from the result of
[
Kem78
]
.
For every
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}
and
x\in\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{C})^{\circ}
, find
(g_{n})\subset\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{C})
with
\lim g_{n}.o=x
. Then Theorem
2.12
applies and completes the proof.

Now assume moreover that
\bm{\mathrm{Z}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}}^{\circ})^{\circ}\subset\bm{\mathrm{H}}
. Let
(g_{n})
and
x
be the same as above. Thus
(g_{n})
is unbounded modulo
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
, and under our assumption, this in turn implies that
(g_{n})
is also unbounded modulo the stabilizer of
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}
. Note that identity component of the normalizer of a connected reductive group (taken to be
\bm{\mathrm{H}}^{\circ}
here) is the same as the identity connected component of the group generated by its centralizer and itself. So
(\Ad(g_{n}).\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}})
diverges and we conclude by Theorem
2.12
.

When
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
may not be reductive, we still claim that
\bm{\mathrm{0}}\notin\overline{\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{C}).\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}}
, which is sufficient by discussion above.
Find a Levi decomposition over
\mathbb{Q}
:
\mathfrak{g}=\mathfrak{l}\ltimes\mathfrak{u}
such that
\mathfrak{h}\subset\mathfrak{l}
. Here
\mathfrak{u}
denotes the Lie algebra of the unipotent radical
\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and
\mathfrak{l}
is the Lie algebra of a maximal reductive subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{L}}
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.

Decompose
\wedge^{\dim\bm{\mathrm{H}}}\mathfrak{g}=\wedge^{\dim\bm{\mathrm{H}}}\mathfrak{l}\oplus W
where

W:=\left\{v\in\wedge^{\dim\bm{\mathrm{H}}}\mathfrak{g}\;\middle|\;v\wedge\bm{\mathrm{u}}=\bm{\mathrm{0}},\;\forall\,\bm{\mathrm{u}}\in\wedge^{\dim\mathfrak{u}}\mathfrak{u}\right\}.

For an element
g\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{C})
, written as
g=u\cdot l
with
u\in\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\mathbb{C})
and
l\in\bm{\mathrm{L}}(\mathbb{C})
, we have

\Ad(g)\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}=\Ad(ul)\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}=\Ad(l)\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}+w

for some
w\in W
.
Hence
\left\lVert\Ad(ul)\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\right\rVert
is bounded from below by some positive multiple of
\left\lVert\Ad(l)\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}\right\rVert
.
But
\Ad(\bm{\mathrm{L}}(\mathbb{C}))\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}
is closed and can never approach
0
. So our claim follows.
∎

Therefore, if
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
are both semisimple and the centralizer of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is finite, the log anti-canonical bundle of
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
is big by
[
HTT15
, Proposition 5.1]
.
The reductivity assumption on
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
can not be dropped (see Example
2.15
below). Also, in the second part, the assumption
\bm{\mathrm{Z}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}}^{\circ})^{\circ}\subset\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is necessary.
Nevertheless, we have

Theorem 2.14
.

Let
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q}(i))
be an arithmetic subgroup of
\Res_{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Q}(i)}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
and
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}
. Assume that for some
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q}(i))
and sequence
(\gamma_{n})
in
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}
one has
\lim_{n\to\infty}\gamma_{n}.x\in\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{C})^{\circ}
, then
d_{\alpha}\geq 1
.
Assume additional that
(\gamma_{n})
can be chosen to be unbounded modulo
\bm{\mathrm{N}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}})^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}(\mathbb{C})\cap\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}
, then
d_{\alpha}\geq 2
.

Here
\Res_{\mathbb{Q}/\mathbb{Q}(i)}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
denotes the restriction of scalar of a linear algebraic group
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
(see
[
Spr98
, 11.4,12.4]
), and
\bm{\mathrm{N}}_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\bm{\mathrm{H}})^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}
is the stabilizer of
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}}
under the adjoint action.

Related results are obtained in
[
HT99
, Theorem 2.7]
for bi-equivariant compactifications of the additive groups
\bm{\mathrm{G}}_{a}^{n}
.

Proof.

Take
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{Q}(i))
and let
\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}
be the stabilizer of
x
in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
, which is a subgroup defined over
\mathbb{Q}(i)
. Let
\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}_{x}}\in(\wedge^{\dim\bm{\mathrm{H}}}\mathfrak{g})\otimes_{\mathbb{Q}}\mathbb{Q}(i)
be a
\mathbb{Q}(i)
-vector lifting
\mathfrak{h}_{x}
.
Define
\varphi^{x}_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}
by modifying the definition of
\varphi_{\bm{\mathrm{v}}}
with
o
replaced by
x
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
replaced by
\bm{\mathrm{H}}_{x}
.
Let
\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}
be an arithmetic subgroup of
\Res_{\mathbb{Q}(i)/\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
viewed as a subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{C})
.
Note that
\chi\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}^{-1}
is defined over
\mathbb{Q}(i)
and thus
(\chi\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}^{-1})(\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}})
is discrete in
\mathbb{C}
, implying that
(\chi\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}^{-1})(\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}})
lies in the unit circle of
\mathbb{C}
, that is,
\left\lvert\chi(\gamma)\Delta_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}}(\gamma)^{-1}\right\rvert=1
for
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}}
.
As
\Ad(\Gamma_{\mathbb{C}})\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{\mathfrak{h}_{x}}
is discrete in
\wedge^{\dim\bm{\mathrm{H}}}\mathfrak{g}\otimes{\mathbb{C}}
, by similar arguments as those used in the previous theorem, we complete the proof.
∎

Example 2.15
.

Let
\bm{\mathrm{G}}:=\SL_{2}
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
be the upper triangular unipotent subgroup. Thus,
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
has an invariant gauge form and can be identified with
\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}\setminus\{0,0\}
. Hence
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
embeds in
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}
as an open and dense subvariety.
The action of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
on
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
extends to
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathbb{Q}}^{2}
.

Let
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
be the blow-up of
\bm{\mathrm{P}}^{2}_{\mathbb{Q}}
at
\{[0:0:1]\}
, then
\bm{\mathrm{X}}=\bm{\mathrm{U}}\sqcup\bm{\mathrm{P}}\sqcup\bm{\mathrm{E}}
where
\bm{\mathrm{P}}
is the proper transform of a hyperplane section and
\bm{\mathrm{E}}
is the exceptional divisor.
By explicit computation,
-\divisor(\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}})=-\bm{\mathrm{E}}+3\bm{\mathrm{P}}
. Therefore, not all
d_{\alpha}
’s are positive here.
Nevertheless, one can verify that
-\divisor(\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}})
is still an effective divisor. Indeed,
-\bm{\mathrm{E}}
is linearly equivalent to the proper transform of
\{y=0\}
.
It is actually big, see
[
FZ13
]
for a general statement.

2.4.
Analytic Clemens complex

Keep the assumptions from last section.
Also assume that
\max\{d_{\alpha}\}>0
.
As a reference, see
[
CLT10
, Section 3.1]
. Since we are assuming
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
to be a simple normal crossing divisor rather than just normal crossing, the discussions are simplified. For instance, if
\sigma(\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha})\neq\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}
for
\sigma_{\neq\id}\in\Gal(\mathbb{C}/\mathbb{R})
, then
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{C})\cap\bm{\mathrm{X}}(\mathbb{R})=\emptyset
.

Let the analytic irreducible components of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}(\mathbb{R})
be indexed as
(D_{\alpha})
for
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\an}
.
There is a natural map from
\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{R}}^{\an}
to
\mathscr{A}
.
For a subset
I\subset\mathscr{A}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R}}
, let
D_{I}:=\cap_{\alpha\in I}D_{\alpha}
.
Since
\mathrm{G}:=\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}
is connected, each connected component of
D_{I}
is
\mathrm{G}
-invariant.

We define a partially ordered set by

(8)

\displaystyle\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R}}:=\left\{(I,Z)\;\middle|\;I\subset\mathscr{A}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R}},\,Z\text{ is a connected component of }D_{I}\neq\emptyset\right\},

\displaystyle(I,Z)\prec(I^{\prime},Z^{\prime})\iff I\subset I^{\prime},\;Z^{\prime}\subset Z.

For
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
, let

\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{R},x}^{\an}:=\left\{\alpha\in\mathscr{A}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R}}\;\middle|\;D_{\alpha}\cap\overline{\mathrm{G}.x}\neq\emptyset\right\}.

And
\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R},x}
is defined similarly as
\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Note that
\mathrm{G}.x
is exactly the connected component of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
containing
x
.

Take
\bm{\mathrm{L}}:=\sum_{\alpha\in\mathscr{A}}\lambda_{\alpha}\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{\alpha}
to be another divisor defined over
\mathbb{R}
such that
\lambda_{\alpha}\geq 0
for all
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}
and

d_{\alpha}>0\implies\lambda_{\alpha}>0.

Thus points of bounded heights (associated with
\bm{\mathrm{L}}
) have finite volume. For every
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\lambda_{\alpha}>0
for some
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{R},x}^{\an}
, let

a_{x}:=\max_{\alpha\in\mathscr{A}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R},x},\,\lambda_{\alpha}\neq 0}\left\{\frac{d_{\alpha}-1}{\lambda_{\alpha}}\right\},

and

\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R},x}(\bm{\mathrm{L}}):=\left\{(I,Z)\in\mathscr{C}_{\mathbb{R},x}^{\an}\;\middle|\;\frac{d_{\alpha}-1}{\lambda_{\alpha}}=a_{x},\,\forall\,\alpha\in I\right\}.

Also let
b_{x}:=\dim\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R},x}(\bm{\mathrm{L}})
.

Let
\Vol
denote the measure on
\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
induced from
\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\chi}
.
Let
\Ht_{\bm{\mathrm{L}}}
be the height at
\infty
associated with certain smooth metric on
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{L}})
together with its canonical section.
For
R>0
, let
B_{R,x}:=\left\{y\in\mathrm{G}.x,\;\Ht(y)<R\right\}
.
The following is proved in

[
CLT10
, Theorem 4.7, Corollary 4.8]
:

Theorem 2.16
.

Take
x\in\bm{\mathrm{U}}(\mathbb{R})
such that
\lambda_{\alpha}>0
for some
\alpha\in\mathscr{A}_{\mathbb{R},x}^{\an}
.
Assume
a_{x}\geq 0
and
b_{x}\geq 1
.
As
R
tends to infinity, there exists
c_{x}>0
such that

\Vol(B_{R,x})\sim\begin{cases}c_{x}R^{a_{x}}\log(R)^{b_{x}-1}\quad&a_{x}\neq 0\\
c_{x}\log(R)^{b_{x}}\quad&a_{x}=0.\end{cases}

The family of probability measures on
\bm{\mathrm{X}}(\mathbb{R})

\nu_{R,x}:=\frac{\bm{\mathrm{1}}_{B_{R,x}}\cdot\Vol}{\Vol(B_{R,x})}

has a limit
\nu
under the weak-
*
topology.
Furthermore,
\nu
is a sum of smooth measures on
Z
as
(I,Z)
varies over faces of
\mathscr{C}^{\an}_{\mathbb{R},x}(\bm{\mathrm{L}})
of dimension
b_{x}
.

3.
Equidistribution and Orbital Counting

In this section, we explain the relationship between equidistribution and orbital counting. Compared to
[
DRS93
,
EM93
]
, the possibility of “focusing” is also considered. On the other hand, we put more restrictions on the height functions, which do not apply to those coming from Riemannian metrics on symmetric spaces.

3.1.
Orbital counting follows from equidistribution

Let
G
be a locally compact second countable topological group and
H
be a closed subgroup. Let
\Gamma
be a discrete subgroup of
G
,
\mathrm{m}_{G}
(resp.
\mathrm{m}_{H}
) be a left
G
-invariant locally finite measure on
G
(resp. on
H
) and
o\in G/H
denote the identity coset.

Assumption 3.1
.

We assume that

(1)

G/\Gamma
has a
G
-invariant locally finite measure
\mathrm{m}_{[G]}
;

(2)

H\Gamma/\Gamma
is closed in
G/\Gamma
and supports an
H
-invariant locally finite measure
\mathrm{m}_{[H]}
;

(3)

there exists a
G
-invariant locally finite measure
\mathrm{m}_{G/H}
on
G/H
and that the triple
(\mathrm{m}_{G},\mathrm{m}_{H},\mathrm{m}_{G/H})
is compatible, that is,

(9)

\int f(x)\,\mathrm{m}_{G}(x)=\int\int f(gh)\,\mathrm{m}_{H}(h)\,\mathrm{m}_{G/H}([g])

for every compactly supported continuous function
f
on
G
.

Item (
1
) always holds in the arithmetic setting.
By
[
Rag72
, Lemma 1.4]
, item (
3
) above is equivalent to the coincidence of the modular functions
\Delta_{G}
and
\Delta_{H}
on
H
. For instance, if
G
is a semisimple Lie group, then
\Delta_{G}
is trivial and such a
\mathrm{m}_{G/H}
exists iff
\Delta_{H}
is trivial.

Let
l:G/H\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
be a continuous function and for a positive number
R
, define

\displaystyle B_{R}:=

\displaystyle\left\{x\in G/H\;|\;l(x)\leq R\right\}.

If we wish to emphasize the role of
l
, an upper index will be added
B_{R}^{l}
.
Assume that
\mathrm{m}_{G/H}(B_{R})
is finite for each
R
.

A family of
(B_{R})_{R\geq 1}
is said to be
well-rounded
(see
[
EM93
, Proposition 1.3]
) iff
for every
0<\varepsilon<1
, there exists an open neighborhood
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}
of
\id\in G
such that

(10)

(1-\varepsilon)\mathrm{m}_{G/H}\left(\bigcup_{g\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}}gB_{R}\right)\leq\mathrm{m}_{G/H}(B_{R})\leq(1+\varepsilon)\mathrm{m}_{G/H}\left(\bigcap_{g\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}}gB_{R}\right).

Remark 3.2
.

This is different from the notion of (two-sided) well-roundedness as introduced in
[
GN12
]
which does not seem to hold in many examples considered in this paper.

Here we assume that
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}
can be arranged such that additionally the following is true for all
R>1
:

(11)

B_{(1-\varepsilon)R}\subset\bigcap_{g\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}}gB_{R}\subset\bigcup_{g\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}}gB_{R}\subset B_{(1+\varepsilon)R}.

This property allows us to prove counting results in the presence of focusing and it is satisfied by all the heights considered in this paper.

A family
(C_{R})_{R\geq 1}
of positive real numbers is said to have a
polynomial asymptotic
if there exist
c_{1}>0
,
c_{2},c_{3}\geq 0
such that
\left(c_{1}R^{c_{2}}(\log{R})^{c_{3}}\right)/C_{R}\to 1
as
R\to+\infty
.

For the application when
\mathrm{m}_{[H]}
is an infinite measure, a weighted variant is also considered. Assume that
\bm{\mathrm{w}}:G/H\to\mathbb{R}_{>0}
satisfies the following:
For every
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an open neighborhood
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}
of
\id\in G
such that

(12)

(1-\varepsilon)\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{gx}\leq\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{x}\leq(1+\varepsilon)\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{gx},\quad\forall x\in G/H,\;\forall g\in\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}.

The weighted orbital counting problem is to study the asymptotic of the function
\Phi^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}}_{R}:G/\Gamma\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\Phi^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}}_{R}([g]):=\frac{1}{C_{R}}\sum_{x\in g\Gamma.o\cap B_{R}}\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{x}

as
R
tends to
+\infty
.

Theorem 3.3
.

Assume that Assumption
3.1
is met, Equa.(
10
,
11
,
12
) hold and
(C_{R})
has polynomial asymptotic.
Assume further that there exists a nonzero locally finite measure
\mu_{\infty}
on
G/\Gamma
such that in the weak-
*
topology,

(13)

\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{1}{C_{R}}\int_{[g]\in B_{R}}\left(\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{g.o}g_{*}\mathrm{m}_{[H]}\right)\,\mathrm{m}_{G/H}([g])=\mu_{\infty}.

Then there exists a positive continuous function
f_{\infty}
such that
\mu_{\infty}=f_{\infty}\cdot\mathrm{m}_{[G]}
and
\lim_{R\to\infty}\Phi^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}}_{R}([g])=f_{\infty}([g])
for every
[g]\in G/\Gamma
.
Actually, if Equa.(
13
) holds only against compactly supported continuous functions supported in some fixed open neighborhood of the identity coset, then we still have that
\mu_{\infty}=f_{\infty}\cdot\mathrm{m}_{[G]}
when restricted to this neighborhood with
f_{\infty}
continuous and
\lim_{R\to\infty}\Phi^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}}_{R}([g])=f_{\infty}([g])
in this neighborhood.

3.2.
Proof of the theorem

The proof follows similar lines as
[
EM93
, Section 5]
or
[
DRS93
, Section 2]
with some modifications.

3.2.1.
Step 1, limit exists and is continuous.

We first show that the limit
\lim_{R\to\infty}\Phi^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}}_{R}([g_{0}])
exists and is continuous.
Fix
[g_{0}]\in G/\Gamma
,

For
0<\varepsilon<1
, choose
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}
satisfying
Equa.(
10
,
11
,
12
). By shrinking to a smaller one we assume that
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}=\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}^{-1}
.

Then we choose
V^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\subset V_{\varepsilon}\subset\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}
to be two families of open neighborhoods of identity such that the closure of
V^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}
is contained in
V_{\varepsilon}
and

\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\mu_{\infty}(V_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[g_{0}])}{\mu_{\infty}(V_{\varepsilon}[g_{0}])}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\frac{\mathrm{m}_{[G]}(V_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[g_{0}])}{\mathrm{m}_{[G]}(V_{\varepsilon}[g_{0}])}=1.

Also choose a continuous function
f_{\varepsilon}
with

1_{V^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}[g_{0}]}\leq f_{\varepsilon}\leq 1_{V_{\varepsilon}[g_{0}]}
.

By an unfolding and folding argument as in
[
DRS93
,
EM93
]
, we have

\displaystyle\langle\Phi^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}}_{R},f_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\mathrm{m}_{[G]}}:=

\displaystyle\int_{x\in G/\Gamma}\Phi^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}}_{R}(x)f_{\varepsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{m}_{[G]}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{R}}\int_{[g]\in B_{R}}\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{g.o}\left(\int_{x\in G/\Gamma}f_{\varepsilon}(gx)\mathrm{m}_{[H]}(x)\right)\mathrm{m}_{G/H}([g])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{x\in G/\Gamma}f_{\varepsilon}(x)\mu_{R}(x)

where

\mu_{R}:=\frac{1}{C_{R}}\int_{[g]\in B_{R}}\left(\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{g.o}g_{*}\mathrm{m}_{[H]}\right)\mathrm{m}_{G/H}([g]).

Therefore,

(14)

\displaystyle\limsup_{R\to\infty}\langle\Phi_{R}^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}},1_{V_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[g_{0}]}\rangle_{\mathrm{m}_{[G]}}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{R\to\infty}\langle\Phi_{R}^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}},f_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\mathrm{m}_{[G]}}

\displaystyle=\int f_{\varepsilon}(x)\mu_{\infty}(x)\leq\liminf_{R\to\infty}\langle\Phi_{R}^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}},1_{V_{\varepsilon}[g_{0}]}\rangle_{\mathrm{m}_{[G]}}.

Also note that

(15)

\mu_{\infty}(V_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[g_{0}])\leq\int f_{\varepsilon}(x)\mu_{\infty}(x)\leq\mu_{\infty}(V_{\varepsilon}[g_{0}]).

Assume
\varepsilon
to be small enough so that the natural map
\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}[g_{0}]
is a homeomorphism. Then
\lambda_{0}\mathrm{m}_{G}|_{\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}}
is identified with
\mathrm{m}_{[G]}|_{\mathcal{O}_{\varepsilon}[g_{0}]}
under this homeomorphism for some
\lambda_{0}>0
.

Now we can start to estimate:

\displaystyle\langle\Phi_{R}^{\bm{\mathrm{w}}},1_{V_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[g_{0}]}\rangle_{\mathrm{m}_{[G]}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{R}}\int_{[g]\in V_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[g_{0}]}\left(\sum_{x\in g\Gamma.o\cap B_{R}}\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{x}\right)\,\mathrm{m}_{[G]}([g])

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{C_{R}}\int_{g\in V_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}\left(\sum_{x\in g_{0}\Gamma.o\cap g^{-1}B_{R}}\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{gx}\right)\,\lambda_{0}\mathrm{m}_{G}(g)

\displaystyle(\text{by Equa.}(\ref{equation_weight_function}))\;\geq

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{m}_{[G]}(V_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}[g_{0}])}{(1+\varepsilon)C_{R}}\left(\sum_{x\in g_{0}\Gamma.o\cap(\cap_{g\in V^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}}g^{-1}B_{R})}\bm{\mathrm{w}}_{x}\right)

\displaystyle(\text{by Equa.}(\ref{equation_strong_well_rounded}))\;\geq
