\lambda=\beta_{1}\widetilde{\lambda},\beta_{2}^{\prime}=\beta_{1}\widetilde{\beta}_{2},y_{1}=\beta_{1}\widetilde{y}_{1},y_{2}^{\prime}=\beta_{1}\widetilde{y}_{2}
;

2.

The ideal of
\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{010^{+}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}
on
\mathrm{U}_{2}
is
\langle\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\widetilde{y}_{2}-\widetilde{\lambda}^{2},\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\widetilde{y}_{1}+\widetilde{y}_{2}\rangle
;

3.

The proper transform of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210}
is contained in the complement of
\mathrm{U}_{2}
;

4.

The proper transform
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{111}^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{2},\widetilde{y}_{2}\rangle
,
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{y}_{1},\widetilde{y}_{2}\rangle
and
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{011}^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{y}_{1},\widetilde{y}_{2},\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\rangle
;

5.

The exceptional divisor
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}
is given by
\langle\beta_{1}\rangle
.

Eliminate
\widetilde{y}_{2}=-\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\widetilde{y}_{1}
by 2. So view
\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{010^{+}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}
on
\mathrm{U}_{2}
as the closed subvariety of
\Spec\mathbb{R}[\beta_{1},\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{2},\widetilde{y}_{1}]
defined by
\langle\widetilde{\beta}_{2}^{2}\widetilde{y}_{1}+\widetilde{\lambda}^{2}\rangle
. The proper transforms of divisors (on
\mathrm{U}_{2}
) become

\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210}^{+}:\emptyset;\;\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{111}^{+}:\langle\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\rangle;\;\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}^{+}:\langle\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{y}_{1}\rangle;\;\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{011}^{+}:\langle\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{2},\widetilde{y}_{1}\rangle;\;\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}:\langle\beta_{1}\rangle.

A.1.7.
\mathrm{U}_{3}

\mathrm{U}_{3}
is identified with

\Spec\mathbb{R}[\beta_{2}^{\prime}]\times\Spec\mathbb{R}[\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{1},\widetilde{y}_{1},\widetilde{y}_{2}]
.
And

1.

\lambda=\beta_{1}\widetilde{\lambda},\beta_{2}^{\prime}=\beta_{1}\widetilde{\beta}_{2},y_{1}=\beta_{1}\widetilde{y}_{1},y_{2}^{\prime}=\beta_{1}\widetilde{y}_{2}
;

2.

The ideal of
\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{010^{+}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}
is
\langle\widetilde{y}_{2}-\widetilde{\lambda}^{2},\widetilde{y}_{1}+\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\widetilde{y}_{2}\rangle
;

3.

The proper transforms of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210},\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{111}
are contained in the complement;

4.

The proper transform
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\lambda}\rangle
;

5.

The exceptional divisor
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}
is given by
\langle\beta_{2}^{\prime}\rangle
.

Eliminating
\widetilde{y}_{2}=\widetilde{\lambda}^{2}
,
\widetilde{y}_{1}=-\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\widetilde{y}_{2}
by 2, we see that
\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{010^{+}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}\cap\mathrm{U}_{3}
is isomorphic to the affine space
\Spec\mathbb{R}[\beta_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{1}]
, with

3’.

The proper transforms of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210},\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{111}
are contained in the complement;

4’.

The proper transform
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\lambda}\rangle
;

5’.

The exceptional divisor
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}
is given by
\langle\beta_{2}^{\prime}\rangle
.

So we have arrived at a smooth pair.

A.1.8.
\mathrm{U}_{4}

\mathrm{U}_{4}
is identified with

\Spec\mathbb{R}[y_{1}]\times\Spec\mathbb{R}[\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{1},\widetilde{\beta}_{2},\widetilde{y}_{2}].

It turns out that
\widetilde{\beta}_{2}=-\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\widetilde{y}_{2}
can be eliminated and we get

1.

\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{010^{+}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}\cap\mathrm{U}_{4}
is a closed subvariety of

\Spec\mathbb{R}[y_{1},\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{1},\widetilde{y}_{2}]
defined by

\langle\widetilde{y}_{2}^{2}\widetilde{\beta}_{1}+\widetilde{\lambda}^{2}\rangle
;

2.

The proper transform of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}
is contained in the complement;

3.

The proper transform
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210}^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\rangle
,

\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{111}^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{y}_{2}\rangle
and

\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{110}^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{1},\widetilde{y}_{2}\rangle
;

4.

The exceptional divisor
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}
is given by
\langle y_{1}\rangle
.

A.1.9.
\mathrm{U}_{5}

\mathrm{U}_{5}
is identified with

\Spec\mathbb{R}[y_{2}^{\prime}]\times\Spec\mathbb{R}[\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{1},\widetilde{\beta}_{2},\widetilde{y}_{1}].

It turns out that both
\widetilde{\beta}_{1}=\widetilde{\lambda}^{2},\widetilde{\beta}_{2}=-\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\widetilde{y}_{1}
can be eliminated and

1.

\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{010^{+}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}
on
\mathrm{U}_{5}
is identified with the affine space
\Spec\mathbb{R}[y_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{y}_{1}]
;

2.

The proper transforms of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}
and
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{111}
are contained in the complement of
\mathrm{U}_{5}
;

3.

The proper transform
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210}^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\lambda}\rangle
.

4.

The exceptional divisor
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}
is
\langle y_{2}^{\prime}\rangle
.

So we also get a smooth pair here.

A.1.10.
Summary

On
\mathrm{U}_{1},\mathrm{U}_{3},\mathrm{U}_{5}
, our
\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{0,1,0^{+}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}
is already a smooth pair. Below is the intersection patterns of the proper transforms of various boundary components:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{111}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{110}^{+}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{011}^{+}}

where the arrow means “is contained in”, or more precisely, “ is the intersection of ”.
The exceptional divisor
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}
, which does not appear, intersects all of them transversally.

A.1.11.

\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{1}:=\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{0,1,0^{+}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}
.

\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{+}_{011}\bigsqcup\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{+}_{110}
on
\mathrm{U}_{2}
and
\mathrm{U}_{4}

\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{1}_{2}
(resp.
\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{2}_{2}
) the exceptional divisor corresponding to
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{+}_{011}
(resp.
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{+}_{110}
).

(resp.
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{111}^{++}
,
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210}^{++}\cap\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{2}_{2}
).

\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}^{++}
is
\langle y_{1}\rangle
and
(\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{2})^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}\rangle
.
The exceptional divisor
\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{1}_{3}
is
\langle\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}\rangle
.

A.1.27.
\mathrm{U}_{431}

Over
\mathrm{U}_{43}
, we are going to have
\mathrm{U}_{431}\cup\mathrm{U}_{432}\cup\mathrm{U}_{433}
, with the ideal
\langle\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}},\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1},\widetilde{y}_{2}\rangle
of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{++}_{210}\cap\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{2}
being blown up.

\mathrm{U}_{431}
is identified as

\Spec\mathbb{R}[y_{1},\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}]\times\Spec\mathbb{R}[\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}}_{1},\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}]

and we have

1.

The ideal of
\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{C}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{2}
is

\langle\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}}_{1}+1\rangle
;

2.

The proper transforms of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{++}_{210}
,
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{111}^{++}
,
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}^{++}
and
(\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{2})^{+}
are in the complement of
\mathrm{U}_{221}
;

3.

The proper transform
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}^{++}
is
\langle y_{1}\rangle
.
The exceptional divisor
\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{2}_{3}
is
\langle\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}\rangle
.

A.1.28.
\mathrm{U}_{432}

\mathrm{U}_{432}
is identified as

\Spec\mathbb{R}[\widetilde{y}_{1},\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1}]\times\Spec\mathbb{R}[\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}},\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}]
.
One can eliminate
\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}=-\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}^{2}
here after which we have

1.

\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{C}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{2}
is identified with the affine space
\Spec\mathbb{R}[\widetilde{y}_{1},\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1},\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}]
;

2.

The proper transforms of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{++}_{210}
,
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{111}^{++}
and
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}^{++}
are in the complement of
\mathrm{U}_{432}
;

3.

The proper transform
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}^{++}
is
\langle y_{1}\rangle
,
(\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{2})^{+}
is
\langle\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}\rangle
.
The exceptional divisor
\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{0}_{3}
is
\langle\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1}\rangle
.

A.1.29.
\mathrm{U}_{433}

\mathrm{U}_{433}
is identified with

\Spec\mathbb{R}[\widetilde{y}_{1},\widetilde{y}_{2}]\times\Spec\mathbb{R}[\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}},\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}}_{1}]

and we eliminate
\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}}_{1}=-\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}^{2}
here.
After that,

1.

\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{C}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{2}
is identified with the affine space
\Spec\mathbb{R}[\widetilde{y}_{1},\widetilde{y}_{2},\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}]
;

2.

The proper transforms of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{++}_{111}
,
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}^{++}
and
(\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{1})^{+}
are in the complement of
\mathrm{U}_{433}
.
The proper transform
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210}^{+++}
is
\langle\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}\rangle
;

3.

The proper transform
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}^{++}
is
\langle y_{1}\rangle
.
The exceptional divisor
\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{2}_{3}
is
\langle\widetilde{y}_{2}\rangle
.

Finally, we have obtained a smooth pair everywhere. Let
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{3}:=\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{C}}}\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{2}
. Since each center of the blowup is
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-invariant, all the blowup morphisms above are
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-equivariant.
Here is an illustration of the intersection pattern. As above,
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}^{++}
intersects everything transversally.

A.1.30.
Explicit invariant gauge form

First we present the invariant gauge form on
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. In coordinates, elements in

\SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
can be written as

(41)

\left(\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}u_{1}&u_{2}\\
u_{3}&u_{4}\end{array}\right],\,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}w_{1}&w_{2}\\
w_{3}&w_{4}\end{array}\right]\right),\text{ satisfying }u_{1}u_{4}-u_{2}u_{3}=w_{1}w_{4}-w_{2}w_{3}=1.

Then the invariant form is given by

\omega_{0}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}u_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}u_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}u_{3}}{u_{1}}\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d}w_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}w_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}w_{3}}{w_{1}}.

Let us put one more restriction:

(42)

f=u_{1}w_{4}+u_{4}w_{1}-u_{2}w_{3}-u_{3}w_{2}=0,

which is invariant under the
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\SL_{2}(\mathbb{R})
action:

(g,h)\cdot(A,B):=(gAh^{-1},gBh^{-1}).

One can verify that

\omega_{1}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}u_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}u_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}u_{3}\wedge\mathrm{d}w_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}w_{2}}{(u_{1}w_{2}-u_{2}w_{1})u_{1}}

satisfies

\omega_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}f=\omega_{0}\text{ restricted to }f=0.

And hence
\omega_{1}
is an invariant volume form on
\{f=0\}
.

Let
\bm{\mathrm{u}}^{\prime}:=(u^{\prime}_{1},u^{\prime}_{2},u^{\prime}_{3},u^{\prime}_{4}):=(u_{1},-u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})
and

\bm{\mathrm{w}}^{\prime}:=(w^{\prime}_{1},w^{\prime}_{2},w^{\prime}_{3},w^{\prime}_{4}):=(w_{1},-w_{2},w_{3},w_{4})
, then Equa.(
41
,
42
) become

(43)

Q_{2}^{0}(\bm{\mathrm{u}}^{\prime},\bm{\mathrm{u}}^{\prime})=Q_{2}^{0}(\bm{\mathrm{w}}^{\prime},\bm{\mathrm{w}}^{\prime})=2,\;Q_{2}^{0}(\bm{\mathrm{u}}^{\prime},\bm{\mathrm{w}}^{\prime})=0.

Let us further set

\bm{\gamma}:=\frac{\bm{\mathrm{u}}^{\prime}+i\bm{\mathrm{w}}^{\prime}}{2},\;\bm{\theta}:=\frac{\bm{\mathrm{u}}^{\prime}-i\bm{\mathrm{w}}^{\prime}}{2}.

Then Equa.(
43
) becomes

Q_{2}^{0}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\gamma})=Q_{2}^{0}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\theta})=0,\;Q_{2}^{0}(\bm{\gamma},\bm{\theta})=1,

which is exactly the model of homogeneous space discussed above in Section
A.1.1
.
Using these new variables,

\omega_{1}=\frac{\mathrm{d\gamma}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d\theta}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d\gamma}_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d\theta}_{2}\wedge(\mathrm{d\gamma}_{3}+\mathrm{d\theta}_{3})}{(\gamma_{1}+\theta_{1})(\gamma_{1}\theta_{2}-\gamma_{2}\theta_{1})}.

Recall the coordinates as introduced in Equa.(
38
) and

[\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}\gamma_{1}&\theta_{1}\\
\gamma_{2}&\theta_{2}\\
\gamma_{3}&\theta_{3}\\
\gamma_{4}&\theta_{4}\end{array}\right]:1]=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\beta_{1}\\
\alpha_{2}&\beta_{2}\\
x_{1}+\alpha_{2}y_{1}&\beta_{1}x_{1}+\beta_{2}y_{1}\\
x_{2}+\alpha_{2}y_{2}&\beta_{1}x_{1}+\beta_{2}y_{2}\end{array}\right]:\lambda].

We have

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\gamma_{1}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\implies\mathrm{d\gamma}_{1}=-\lambda^{-1}\mathrm{d\lambda}\\
\theta_{1}=\frac{\beta_{1}}{\lambda}\implies\mathrm{d\theta}_{1}=\pm\lambda^{-1}\mathrm{d\beta}_{1}\;\mod\,\mathrm{d\lambda}\\
\gamma_{2}=\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\lambda}\implies\mathrm{d\gamma}_{2}=\lambda^{-1}\mathrm{d\alpha}_{2}\;\mod\,\mathrm{d\lambda}\\
\theta_{2}=\frac{\beta_{2}}{\lambda}\implies\mathrm{d\theta}_{2}=\lambda^{-1}\mathrm{d\beta}_{2}^{\prime}\mod\,\mathrm{d\lambda},\,\mathrm{d\beta}_{1},\,\mathrm{d\alpha}_{2},\end{cases}

and finally,

\displaystyle\gamma_{3}+\theta_{3}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}(x_{1}+\alpha_{2}y_{1}+\beta_{1}x_{1}+\beta_{2}y_{1})

\displaystyle\implies\mathrm{d\gamma}_{3}+\mathrm{d\theta}_{3}=\pm\frac{1+\beta_{1}}{\lambda}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\;\mod\mathrm{d\lambda},\,\mathrm{d\beta}_{1},\,\mathrm{d\alpha}_{2},\,\mathrm{d\beta}_{2}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}+\theta_{1}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}(1+\beta_{1}),\quad\gamma_{1}\theta_{2}-\gamma_{2}\theta_{1}=\lambda^{-2}\beta_{2}^{\prime}.

In sum, we get the invariant gauge form on
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}
:

\omega_{1}=\pm\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}\beta_{2}^{\prime}}\mathrm{d\lambda}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{2}^{\prime}\wedge\mathrm{d}x_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{2}.

Similar to the above, we can ignore the variables
x_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
and focus on

(44)

\omega_{2}:=\frac{1}{\lambda^{3}\beta_{2}^{\prime}}\mathrm{d\lambda}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{2}^{\prime}.

We will compute poles of
\omega_{2}
, or equivalently,
\omega_{1}
, along the boundaries in
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{3}
.

A.1.31.
The order of pole along
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}^{++}
is 2

This can be checked on
\mathrm{U}_{1}
where

\beta_{1}=\lambda\widetilde{\beta}_{1},\;\beta_{2}^{\prime}=\lambda\widetilde{\beta}_{2}^{\prime},\;\widetilde{\beta}_{1}=-\widetilde{\beta}_{2}^{2}\widetilde{y}_{1}.

In particular,

\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\beta}_{1}=-\widetilde{\beta}_{2}^{2}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{y}_{1}\mod\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\beta}_{2}.

Consequently,

(45)

\omega_{2}=\frac{\widetilde{\beta}_{2}}{\lambda^{2}}\mathrm{d\lambda}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{y}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\beta}_{2}.

Since the local equation of
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1}
is given by
\lambda=0
, we get

\Pole(\omega_{2},\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{1})=2
. But the blowup morphisms are trivial over
\mathrm{U}_{1}
hence
\Pole(\omega_{2},\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{++}_{1})=2
.

A.1.32.
The order of pole along
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{022}^{+++}
is 3

This can be found on
\mathrm{U}_{3}
where

\lambda=\widetilde{\lambda}\beta_{2}^{\prime},\;\beta_{1}=\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\beta_{2}^{\prime}.

Thus,

\omega_{2}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\lambda}^{3}\beta_{2}^{\prime 4}}\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\lambda}\beta_{2}^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\beta_{2}^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{2}^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\lambda}^{3}(\beta_{2}^{\prime})^{2}}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\lambda}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{2}^{\prime}.

Since the local equation of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{+}_{022}
is given by
\widetilde{\lambda}=0
, we have
\Pole(\omega_{2},\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{+}_{022})=3
. But the blowup morphism is trivial over
\mathrm{U}_{3}
, so
\Pole(\omega_{2},\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{+++}_{022})=3
.

A.1.33.
The order of pole along
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210}^{+++}
is 1

This can be checked on
\mathrm{U}_{5}
where one has the coordinates
y_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{y}_{1},\widetilde{\lambda}
and
\widetilde{\beta}_{2}=\widetilde{\lambda}^{2}
,
\widetilde{\beta}_{1}=-\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\widetilde{y}_{1}=-\widetilde{\lambda}^{2}\widetilde{y}_{1}
are eliminated. Also,
\lambda=\widetilde{\lambda}y_{2}^{\prime}
,
\beta_{1}=\widetilde{\beta}_{1}y_{2}^{\prime}
and
\beta_{2}=\widetilde{\beta}_{2}y_{2}^{\prime}
.
The local equation of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{+}_{210}
is
\widetilde{\lambda}=0
.
Then one can compute

\displaystyle\omega_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{y_{2}^{\prime 4}\widetilde{\lambda}^{3}\widetilde{\beta}_{2}}\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\lambda}y_{2}^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\beta}_{1}y_{2}^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\beta}_{2}y_{2}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{(y_{2}^{\prime})^{2}\widetilde{\lambda}}\mathrm{d}y_{2}^{\prime}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{y}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\lambda}.

Now one sees directly that
\Pole(\omega_{2},\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210}^{+++})=\Pole(\omega_{2},\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{210}^{+})=1
.

A.1.34.
The order of pole along
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{3}^{0}
is 5, along
(\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{1})^{+}
is 7

These can be checked on
\mathrm{U}_{222}
.

First we have that on
\mathrm{U}_{2}
, the coordinates are given by

\beta_{1},\widetilde{\lambda},\widetilde{\beta}_{2},\widetilde{y}_{1}
and
\widetilde{y}_{2}=-\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\widetilde{y}_{1}
is eliminated. The local equation of
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{1}
is

\widetilde{\beta}_{2}^{2}\widetilde{y}_{1}+\widetilde{\lambda}^{2}=0.

And one has the relations:

\lambda=\widetilde{\lambda}\beta_{1},\;\beta_{2}=\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\beta_{1},\;y_{i}=\widetilde{y}_{i}\beta_{1},\;i=1,2.

From this one can compute

\omega_{2}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{\lambda}^{3}\beta_{1}^{2}\widetilde{\beta}_{2}}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\lambda}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\beta}_{2}.

On
\mathrm{U}_{22}
one has coordinates
\beta_{1},\widetilde{\beta}_{2},\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}},\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1}
with

\widetilde{\lambda}=\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}},\;\widetilde{y}_{1}=\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1}
.
Local equation of
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{2}
is

\widetilde{\beta}_{2}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1}+\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}^{2}=0.

Then

\omega_{2}=\frac{1}{(\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}})^{3}\beta_{1}^{2}\widetilde{\beta}_{2}^{3}}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\beta}_{2}.

Finally over
\mathrm{U}_{222}
, we have the coordinates
\beta_{1},\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1},\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}
and
\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{2}=-\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}^{2}
is eliminated. And
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{3}\cap\mathrm{U}_{222}
is the full affine space here. One has
\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}=\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}
,
\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{2}=\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1}\widetilde{\beta}_{2}
.
The local equation of
\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{0}_{3}
is
\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1}=0
and that of
(\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{1})^{+}
is
\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}=0
.
Now compute

\displaystyle\omega_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{3}\beta_{1}^{2}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{2})^{3}(\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1})^{6}}\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}(\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}^{2})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{7}\beta_{1}^{2}(\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1})^{5}}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}.

Hence
\Pole(\omega_{2},\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{0}_{3})=5
and
\Pole(\omega_{2},(\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{1})^{+})=7
.

A.1.35.
The order of pole along
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{3}^{1}
is 3, along
(\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{2})^{+}
is 5

We shall check this on
\mathrm{U}_{422}
.

To cut the story short, on
\mathrm{U}_{422}
, we have coordinates
y_{1},\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2},\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{3}
is the full affine space.
The local equation of
\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{1}_{3}
is
\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}=0
and that of
(\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{2})^{+}
is
\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}=0
.
The relations with the old coordinates are

\displaystyle\lambda=y_{1}\widetilde{\lambda}=y_{1}\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}=-y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{3}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2},

\displaystyle\beta_{1}=-y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{2},\;\;\beta_{2}^{\prime}=-y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{4}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}.

Now we can compute

\displaystyle\omega_{2}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{3}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2})\wedge\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{4}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2})\wedge\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{2})}{y_{1}^{3}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{9}(\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2})^{3}y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{4}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{y_{1}^{4}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{13}(\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2})^{4}}\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{3}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2})\wedge\left(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{3}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}\wedge\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{2})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{y_{1}^{2}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{5}(\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2})^{3}}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}\wedge\mathrm{d}y_{1}.

Hence
\Pole(\omega_{2},\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{1}_{3})=3
and
\Pole(\omega_{2},(\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{2}^{2})^{+})=5
.

A.1.36.
The order of pole along
\bm{\mathrm{E}}_{3}^{2}
is 3

This will be checked on
\mathrm{U}_{432}
.
We have here coordinates
y_{1},\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1},\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}
and there are no further constrains on
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{3}
.
Moreover, the local equation of
\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{2}_{3}
is
\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1}=0
.
The relations with the old coordinates are given by

\displaystyle\lambda=y_{1}\widetilde{\lambda}=y_{1}\widetilde{y}_{2}\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}=y_{1}\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1}^{2}\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}=-y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{2}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{3},

\displaystyle\beta_{1}=y_{1}\widetilde{\beta}_{1}=y_{1}\widetilde{y}_{2}\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1}=-y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{2}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{2},\;

\displaystyle\beta_{2}^{\prime}=y_{1}\widetilde{\beta}_{2}=-y_{1}\widetilde{\beta}_{1}\widetilde{y}_{2}=-y_{1}\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1}\widetilde{y}_{2}^{2}=-y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{3}(\widetilde{\widetilde{y}}_{2})^{2}=-y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{3}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{4}.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\omega_{2}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{2}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{3})\wedge\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{2}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{2})\wedge\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{3}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{4})}{\left(y_{1}^{3}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{6}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{9}\right)\cdot\left(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{3}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{4}\right)}

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{y_{1}^{4}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{9}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{13}}\left(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{2}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{2}\right)\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}\wedge\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{2}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{2})\wedge\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{3}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{4})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{y_{1}^{3}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{7}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{11}}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}\wedge(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{2}\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{2})\wedge(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{4}\mathrm{d}(y_{1}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{3})

\displaystyle=\pm\frac{1}{y_{1}^{2}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1})^{3}(\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}})^{5}}\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{\lambda}}}\wedge\mathrm{d}y_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{\widetilde{\beta}}_{1},

from which one sees that
\Pole(\omega_{2},\bm{\mathrm{E}}^{2}_{3})=3
.

A.2.
Example II

Here we provide details for the example presented in Section
8
.
Recall that

\bm{\mathrm{X}}
is the Zariski closure of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}:=\bm{\mathrm{G}}.y_{0}
with
y_{0}:=([\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1}:1],[\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1}^{\vee}:1])
in
\bm{\mathrm{P}}^{n+1}_{\mathbb{Q}}\times\bm{\mathrm{P}}^{n+1}_{\mathbb{Q}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{D}}
is the complement of
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
in
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
.
If a general (closed) point in
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
is denoted as
[\bm{\mathrm{v}}:s],[\bm{\alpha}:t]
, then

\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}
is defined by
s=0
and
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{2}
by
t=0
.

A.2.1.
Local equations of stratum

We define the following affine open subvarieties of
\bm{\mathrm{X}}
:

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{1}:\quad\left\{([1:v_{2}:...:v_{n+1}:s],[\alpha_{1}:1:\alpha_{3}:...:\alpha_{n+1}:t])\right\};

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{2}:\quad\left\{([v_{1}:v_{2}:...:v_{n+1}:1],[1:\alpha_{2}:...:\alpha_{n+1}:t])\right\};

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{3}:\quad\left\{([1:v_{2}:...:v_{n+1}:s],[\alpha_{1}:\alpha_{2}:...:\alpha_{n+1}:1])\right\}.

Using these local coordinates, one finds that
6
6

6

Compared to last subsection, it is direct to verify the ideals generated by
f_{i}
is prime.

Lemma A.4
.

As a closed subvariety of
\Spec\mathbb{Q}[v_{2},...,v_{n+1},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{3},...,\alpha_{n+1},s,t]
, the ideal of
\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{1}
is generated by

f_{1}:=\alpha_{1}+v_{2}+\sum_{i=3}^{n+1}v_{i}\alpha_{i}-st.

The ideal of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\cap\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{1}
(resp.
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{2}\cap\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{1}
) is generated by
f_{1},s
(resp.
f_{1},t
).

As a closed subvariety of
\Spec\mathbb{Q}[v_{1},...,v_{n+1},\alpha_{2},...,\alpha_{n+1},t]
, the ideal of
\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
is generated by

f_{2}:=v_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n+1}v_{i}\alpha_{i}-t.

The ideal of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{2}\cap\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
is generated by
f_{2},t
and

\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\cap\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
is empty.

As a closed subvariety of
\Spec\mathbb{Q}[v_{2},...,v_{n+1},\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{n+1},s]
, the ideal of
\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{3}
is generated by

f_{3}:=\alpha_{1}+\sum_{i=2}^{n+1}v_{i}\alpha_{i}-s.

The ideal of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}\cap\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{3}
is generated by
f_{2},s
and

\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{2}\cap\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{3}
is empty.

A.2.2.
The divisor of the invariant gauge form

First we work in the affine space
\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{n+1}\times\bm{\mathrm{A}}^{n+1}
with coordinates

[v_{1}:...:v_{n+1}:1]\times[\alpha_{1}:...:\alpha_{n+1}:1]
with equation
f=\sum v_{i}\alpha_{i}-1=0
.
In the ambient affine space, the differential form

\omega_{1}:=\mathrm{d}v_{1}\wedge...\wedge\mathrm{d}v_{n+1}\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{1}\wedge...\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{n+1}

is
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-invariant. Then, the unique (up to a scalar) solution
\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{U}}}
on
f=0
to

\omega_{1}=\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{U}}}\wedge\mathrm{d}f

is the invariant gauge form.
So when
\alpha_{1}\neq 0
,

\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{U}}}=\alpha_{1}^{-1}\mathrm{d}v_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}v_{3}\wedge...\wedge\mathrm{d}v_{n+1}\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{1}\wedge...\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{n+1}.

Turning into coordinates on
\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{1}
, we get

\displaystyle\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{U}}}=

\displaystyle(t^{-1})^{-1}\mathrm{d}\frac{1}{s}\wedge\mathrm{d}\frac{v_{3}}{s}\wedge...\wedge\mathrm{d}\frac{v_{n+1}}{s}\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d\alpha}_{1}}{t}\wedge\frac{1}{t}\wedge\frac{\alpha_{3}}{t}\wedge...\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d\alpha}_{n+1}}{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\pm\frac{1}{s^{n+1}t^{n+1}}\mathrm{d}s\wedge\mathrm{d}v_{3}\wedge...\wedge\mathrm{d}v_{n+1}\wedge\mathrm{d}t\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{3}\wedge...\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{n+1}.

Now it is direct to see that

\Pole(\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{U}}},\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1})=\Pole(\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{U}}},\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{2})=n+1.

A.2.3.
Measure compactification

The lemma below follows from the equidistribution theorem of
[
EMS96
]
and the nondivergence theorem of
[
DM91
]
(stated more precisely in
[
DGU21
, Theorem 4.6]
). Note that there are exactly two rational parabolic subgroups containing
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
. And for a sequence
(g_{n})
in
\mathrm{G}
, to test whether adjoint orbits based at the two vectors representing the Lie algebras of these two parabolic subgroups go to
0
, is the same as to test whether
(g_{n}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1})
and
(g_{n}.\bm{\mathrm{e}}^{\vee}_{1})
go to
0
.

Lemma A.5
.

Let
(g_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathrm{G}
. By passing to a subsequence, we are in exactly one of the following situations:

1.

(g_{n}.\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1})
or
(g_{n}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1}^{\vee})
tends to
\bm{\mathrm{0}}
, in which case,

\lim_{n\to\infty}(g_{n})_{*}\mathrm{m}^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}_{[\mathrm{H}]}=\bm{\mathrm{0}};

2.

(g_{n})
converges to
\delta\in\mathrm{G}
modulo
\mathrm{H}
, in which case,

\lim_{n\to\infty}(g_{n})_{*}\mathrm{m}^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}_{[\mathrm{H}]}=\delta_{*}\mathrm{m}^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}_{[\mathrm{H}]};

3.

(g_{n})
is unbounded modulo
\mathrm{H}
, but converges to
\delta\in\mathrm{G}
modulo
\mathrm{M}_{1}
, in which case,

\lim_{n\to\infty}(g_{n})_{*}\mathrm{m}^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}_{[\mathrm{H}]}=\delta_{*}\mathrm{m}^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}_{[\mathrm{M}_{1}]};

4.

(g_{n})
is unbounded modulo
\mathrm{H}
, but converges to
\delta\in\mathrm{G}
modulo
\mathrm{M}_{2}
, in which case,

\lim_{n\to\infty}(g_{n})_{*}\mathrm{m}^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}_{[\mathrm{H}]}=\delta_{*}\mathrm{m}^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}_{[\mathrm{M}_{2}]};

5.

(g_{n}.\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1})
and
(g_{n}.\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1}^{\vee})
both tend to infinity, in which case,

\lim_{n\to\infty}(g_{n})_{*}\mathrm{m}^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}_{[\mathrm{H}]}=\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{G}]}^{\bm{\mathrm{1}}}.

To deduce Lemma
8.6
from Lemma
A.5
, one observes that
(g_{n})
is bounded modulo
\mathrm{M}_{1}
iff
(g_{n}.\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1})
is bounded away from
\bm{\mathrm{0}}
and infinity and similarly, it is bounded modulo
\mathrm{M}_{2}
iff
(g_{n}.\bm{\mathrm{e}}^{\vee}_{1})
is bounded away from
\bm{\mathrm{0}}
and infinity.

A.2.4.
The metric line bundles

Let
\rho
be a non-negative smooth function on
\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
such that

\rho(v)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }\left\lVert v\right\rVert\geq 1\\
1&\text{ if }\left\lVert v\right\rVert\leq 0.5.\end{cases}

For a positive integer
\kappa
, we define a smooth metric
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2\kappa}
on
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
by

\left\lVert\bm{1}_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}}([\bm{\mathrm{v}}:s],[\bm{\alpha}:t])\right\rVert_{2\kappa}:=\left({\frac{s^{2\kappa}}{\sum v_{i}^{2\kappa}+s^{2\kappa}\rho(\frac{v_{1}}{s},...,\frac{v_{n+1}}{s})}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\kappa}}.

Similarly,
define a smooth metric
\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert_{2\kappa}
on
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{2})
by

\left\lVert\bm{1}_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{2}}([\bm{\mathrm{v}}:s],[\bm{\alpha}:t])\right\rVert_{2\kappa}:=\left({\frac{t^{2\kappa}}{\sum\alpha_{i}^{2\kappa}+t^{2\kappa}\rho(\frac{\alpha_{1}}{t},...,\frac{\alpha_{n+1}}{t})}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\kappa}}.

One can check they are indeed smooth metrics using local coordinates. Let us check the one on
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
.
On
\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{1}\cup\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{3}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{1}{s}\bm{1}_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}}([1:v_{2}:...:v_{n+1}:s],[\bm{\alpha}:t])\right\rVert_{2\kappa}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{(1+v_{2}^{2\kappa}+...+v_{n+1}^{2\kappa})+{s}^{2\kappa}\rho(\frac{1}{s},\frac{v_{2}}{s},...,\frac{v_{n+1}}{s})}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\kappa}}.

Note that
\rho
vanishes when
\left\lvert s\right\rvert\leq 1
. On
\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\lVert\bm{1}_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}}([v_{1}:...:v_{n+1}:1],[\bm{\alpha}:t])\right\rVert_{2\kappa}=\left(\frac{1}{\sum v_{i}^{2\kappa}+\rho(v_{1},...,v_{n+1})}\right)^{\frac{1}{2\kappa}}.

Since
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
is generated by
\bm{\mathrm{1}}_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}}
on
\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{2}
and generated by
\frac{1}{s}\bm{\mathrm{1}}_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}}
on
\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{1}\cup\bm{\mathrm{O}}_{3}
, the above computation shows the smoothness of the metric on
\mathcal{O}_{\bm{\mathrm{X}}}(\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1})
. Note that
\rho
vanishes when evaluated on integral points of
\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}
.

A.3.
Example III

Here we provide details for the example presented in Section
9
.
Recall that we are concerned with certain homogeneous variety
\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}\cong\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{H}}
with
\bm{\mathrm{G}}=\SL_{3}
and
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
being the full diagonal torus, which is compactified by

\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}=\left\{(\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{1},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{2},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{3},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{12},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{13},\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{23})\in(\bm{\mathrm{P}}^{2})^{3}\times(\bm{\mathrm{Gr}}_{2,3})^{3}\;\middle|\;\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{I}\subset\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{J},\;\forall\,I\subset J\right\}.

It is equipped with
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-invariant closed subvarieties:

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{1}:=\left\{\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{1}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{2}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{3}\right\},\;\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{2}:=\left\{\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{13}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{13}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{23}\right\},

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{12,3}:=\left\{\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{1}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{2},\,\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{13}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{23}\right\},\;\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{13,2}:=\left\{\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{1}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{3},\,\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{12}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{23}\right\},\;

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{23,1}:=\left\{\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{2}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{3},\,\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{12}=\bm{\mathrm{l}}_{13}\right\}.

A.3.1.
An open affine subvariety

Using Plucker coordinates, we identify
\bm{\mathrm{Gr}}_{2,3}
with
\bm{\mathrm{P}}^{2}
. So
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}
is a closed subvariety of
(\bm{\mathrm{P}}^{2})^{6}
. For our purpose, we may restrict to the open subvariety
\bm{\mathrm{O}}
where all the first coordinates are nonzero. That is, points of the form

\left\{([1:a_{2}:a_{3}],[1:b_{2}:b_{3}],[1:c_{2}:c_{3}],[1:\alpha_{13}:\alpha_{23}],[1:\beta_{13}:\beta_{23}],[1:\gamma_{13}:\gamma_{23}])\right\}

that are contained in
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}
.
Thus,
\bm{\mathrm{O}}
is a closed subvariety of

\Spec\mathbb{R}[a_{2},a_{3},b_{2},b_{3},c_{2},c_{3},\alpha_{13},\alpha_{23},\beta_{13},\beta_{23},\gamma_{13},\gamma_{23}]

defined by the prime ideal generated by (these functions come from the incidence relations)

\displaystyle f_{1}:=\alpha_{23}-a_{2}\alpha_{13}+a_{3},\;f_{2}:=\beta_{23}-a_{2}\beta_{13}+a_{3},\;f_{3}:=\alpha_{23}-b_{2}\alpha_{13}+b_{3},\;

\displaystyle f_{4}:=\gamma_{23}-b_{2}\gamma_{13}+b_{3},\;f_{5}:=\beta_{23}-c_{2}\beta_{13}+c_{3},\;f_{6}:=\gamma_{23}-c_{2}\gamma_{13}+c_{3}.

That they indeed generate a prime ideal will be clear later.

A.3.2.
The invariant gauge form

We compute the invariant gauge form on
\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}
under these coordinates.
Set

\displaystyle\eta_{1}:=\frac{1}{(\gamma_{23}-a_{2}\gamma_{13}+a_{3})^{3}}\mathrm{d}a_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}a_{3}\wedge\mathrm{d\gamma}_{13}\wedge\mathrm{d\gamma}_{23};

\displaystyle\eta_{2}:=\frac{1}{(\beta_{23}-b_{2}\beta_{13}+b_{3})^{3}}\mathrm{d}b_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}b_{3}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{13}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{23};

\displaystyle\eta_{3}:=\frac{1}{(\alpha_{23}-c_{2}\alpha_{13}+c_{3})^{3}}\mathrm{d}c_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}c_{3}\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{13}\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{23}.

Lemma A.6
.

For
i=1,2,3
, each
\eta_{i}
is
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-invariant.

Proof.

We only present the proof for
\eta_{1}
. The other two cases are similar.

We write
\bm{a}
(resp.
\bm{\gamma}
) as a shorthand for
(a_{2},a_{3})
(resp.
(\gamma_{13},\gamma_{23})
). Also set
\mathrm{d}\bm{a}:=\mathrm{d}a_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}a_{3}
and
\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}:=\mathrm{d\gamma}_{13}\wedge\mathrm{d\gamma}_{23}
.
Let
\phi_{1}(\bm{a},\bm{\gamma}):={(\gamma_{23}-a_{2}\gamma_{13}+a_{3})^{3}}
and

\displaystyle g_{1}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&x&0\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right],\,g_{2}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&x\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right],\,g_{3}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
0&1&x\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right],\,g_{4}:=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&0\\
x&1&0\\
y&z&1\end{array}\right].

It suffices to verify that
\eta_{1}
is invariant under
g_{1},g_{2},g_{3},g_{4}
for all
x,y,z
.
We only verify the
g_{2}
-invariance and the other cases are similar.

For functions
f=a_{2},a_{3},\bm{a},\gamma_{13},\gamma_{23}
or
\bm{\gamma}
, let
f^{g}:=g_{2}^{*}(f)
.
Since

\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&x\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right]\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
a_{2}\\
a_{3}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1+xa_{3}\\
a_{2}\\
a_{3}\end{array}\right],

we have

a_{2}^{g}=\frac{a_{2}}{1+xa_{3}},\;a_{3}^{g}=\frac{a_{3}}{1+xa_{3}},\;\text{ and }\mathrm{d}\bm{a}^{g}=(1+xa_{3})^{-3}\mathrm{d}\bm{a}.

Under the basis
(\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{2},\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{1}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{3},\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{2}\wedge\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{3})
, we have

\left(\wedge^{2}\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&x\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right]\right)\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\gamma_{13}\\
\gamma_{23}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{ccc}1&0&-x\\
0&1&0\\
0&0&1\end{array}\right]\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1\\
\gamma_{13}\\
\gamma_{23}\end{array}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c}1-x\gamma_{23}\\
\gamma_{13}\\
\gamma_{23}\end{array}\right].

Thus,

\gamma_{13}^{g}=\frac{\gamma_{13}}{1-x\gamma_{23}},\;\gamma_{23}^{g}=\frac{\gamma_{23}}{1-x\gamma_{23}},\;\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}^{g}=(1-x\gamma_{23})^{-3}\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}.

On the other hand,

\displaystyle\phi_{1}(\bm{a}^{g},\bm{\gamma}^{g})

\displaystyle=(1-x\gamma_{23})^{-1}\gamma_{23}-(1+xa_{3})^{-1}a_{2}\cdot(1-x\gamma_{23})^{-1}\gamma_{13}+(1+xa_{3})^{-1}a_{3}

\displaystyle=(1-x\gamma_{23})^{-1}(1+xa_{3})^{-1}\phi_{1}(\bm{a},\bm{\gamma}).

It follows that

\phi_{1}^{-3}(\bm{a}^{g},\bm{\gamma}^{g})\mathrm{d}\bm{a}^{g}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}^{g}=\phi_{1}^{-3}(\bm{a},\bm{\gamma})\mathrm{d}\bm{a}\wedge\mathrm{d}\bm{\gamma}.

That is to say,
\eta_{1}
is
g_{2}
-invariant.
∎

It can be checked that
f_{i}
’s intersect transversally in an open set. Thus, the solution
\omega
to the equation

(46)

\eta_{1}\wedge\eta_{2}\wedge\eta_{3}=\omega\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{1}\wedge...\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{6}

is unique when restricted to the common zero set of
f_{i}
’s. As
\eta_{i}
’s and
\mathrm{d}f_{i}
’s are
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-invariant. It follows that such an
\omega
would also be
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
-invariant.

Lemma A.7
.

Let

\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}}:=\frac{\mathrm{d}a_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}a_{3}\wedge\mathrm{d}b_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}c_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{13}\wedge\mathrm{d\gamma}_{13}}{(a_{2}-b_{2})^{6}(\alpha_{13}-\gamma_{13})^{3}(\beta_{13}-\alpha_{13})^{6}(a_{2}-c_{2})^{4}}.

Then
\omega:=\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}}
solves Equa.(
46
) above.

Proof.

Note that by
f_{1}=...=f_{6}=0
, we have

\displaystyle\gamma_{23}-a_{2}\gamma_{13}+a_{3}

\displaystyle=b_{2}\gamma_{13}-b_{3}-a_{2}\gamma_{13}+a_{3}=b_{2}\gamma_{13}+\alpha_{23}-b_{2}\alpha_{13}-a_{2}\gamma_{13}+a_{3}

\displaystyle=(b_{2}-a_{2})\gamma_{13}+(a_{2}-b_{2})\alpha_{13}=(a_{2}-b_{2})(\alpha_{13}-\gamma_{13});

\displaystyle\beta_{23}-b_{2}\beta_{13}+b_{3}

\displaystyle=a_{2}\beta_{13}-a_{3}-b_{2}\beta_{13}+b_{2}\alpha_{13}-\alpha_{23}

\displaystyle=(a_{2}-b_{2})\beta_{13}-a_{3}+b_{2}\alpha_{13}-a_{2}\alpha_{13}+a_{3}=(a_{2}-b_{2})(\beta_{13}-\alpha_{13});

\displaystyle\alpha_{23}-c_{2}\alpha_{13}+c_{3}

\displaystyle=a_{2}\alpha_{13}-a_{3}-c_{2}\alpha_{13}+c_{2}\beta_{13}-\beta_{23}

\displaystyle=(a_{2}-c_{2})\alpha_{13}+c_{2}\beta_{13}-a_{2}\beta_{13}=(a_{2}-c_{2})(\alpha_{13}-\beta_{13}).

And modulo (the kernel of)
\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}}\wedge\cdot
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}f_{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{3}\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{4}\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{5}\wedge\mathrm{d}f_{6}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\mathrm{d\alpha}_{23}\wedge(\mathrm{d\beta}_{23}-a_{2}\mathrm{d\beta}_{13})\wedge\mathrm{d}b_{3}\wedge\mathrm{d\gamma}_{23}\wedge(-c_{2}\mathrm{d\beta}_{13}+\mathrm{d\beta}_{23})\wedge\mathrm{d}c_{3}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle(a_{2}-c_{2})\mathrm{d\alpha}_{23}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{23}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}_{13}\wedge\mathrm{d}b_{3}\wedge\mathrm{d\gamma}_{23}\wedge\mathrm{d}c_{3}.

Putting these computations together yields the result.
∎

A.3.3.
New coordinates

We perform a few change of variables and eliminate a few redundant variables to make the description of
\bm{\mathrm{O}}
clearer. The explicit formula of the group action in the new coordinates will be quite complicated but we are not concerned about it.

Using
f_{1},f_{2},f_{3},f_{4},f_{5}=0
, we can eliminate

\begin{cases}\alpha_{23}=a_{2}\alpha_{13}-a_{3},\\
\beta_{23}=a_{2}\beta_{13}-a_{3},\\
b_{3}=b_{2}\alpha_{13}-a_{2}\alpha_{13}+a_{3},\\
\gamma_{23}=b_{2}\gamma_{13}-b_{2}\alpha_{13}+a_{2}\alpha_{13}-a_{3},\\
c_{3}=c_{2}\beta_{13}-a_{2}\beta_{13}+a_{3}\end{cases}

And
f_{6}=0
is equivalent to

\displaystyle(b_{2}\gamma_{13}-b_{2}\alpha_{13}+a_{2}\alpha_{13}-a_{3})-c_{2}\gamma_{13}+(c_{2}\beta_{13}-a_{2}\beta_{13}+a_{3})=0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle b_{2}(\gamma_{13}-\alpha_{13})+a_{2}(\alpha_{13}-\beta_{13})+c_{2}(\beta_{13}-\gamma_{13})=0

\displaystyle\iff

\displaystyle-(a_{2}-c_{2})(\beta_{13}-\alpha_{13})+(b_{2}-c_{2})(\gamma_{13}-\alpha_{13})=0.

Now we replace
\beta_{13},\gamma_{13},a_{2}^{\prime}
and
b_{2}^{\prime}
by

\beta_{13}^{\prime}:=\beta_{13}-\alpha_{13},\;\gamma_{13}^{\prime}:=\gamma_{13}-\alpha_{13},\;a_{2}^{\prime}:=a_{2}-c_{2},\;b_{2}^{\prime}:=b_{2}-c_{2}.

Hence the open subvariety
\bm{\mathrm{O}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{\INC}
is identified with the
zero set of the prime ideal

\langle-a_{2}^{\prime}\beta_{13}^{\prime}+\gamma_{13}^{\prime}b_{2}^{\prime}\rangle\text{ in }\Spec\mathbb{Q}[a_{3},c_{2},\alpha_{13}]\times\Spec\mathbb{Q}[a_{2}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\beta_{13}^{\prime},\gamma_{13}^{\prime}].

Thus
\bm{\mathrm{O}}
is singular exactly when
a_{2}^{\prime}=\beta_{13}^{\prime}=\gamma_{13}^{\prime}=b_{2}^{\prime}=0
.
The first three coordinates
a_{3},c_{2},\alpha_{13}
have no effect on the discussions below and are often omitted for simplicity.

A.3.4.
Invariant gauge form in the new coordinates

Under the new coordinates, we have

\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}}=\pm(\mathrm{d}a_{3}\wedge\mathrm{d}c_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d\alpha}_{13})\wedge\frac{\mathrm{d}a_{2}^{\prime}\wedge\mathrm{d}b_{2}^{\prime}\wedge\mathrm{d\gamma}_{13}^{\prime}}{(a_{2}^{\prime}-b_{2}^{\prime})^{6}(\gamma_{13}^{\prime})^{3}(\beta_{13}^{\prime})^{6}(a_{2}^{\prime})^{4}}.

A.3.5.
Boundary stratum in the new coordinates

We list the ideal for
\bm{\mathrm{O}}
and various boundary stratum intersecting
\bm{\mathrm{O}}
:

\text{The ideal of }\begin{cases}\bm{\mathrm{O}}\text{ is generated by }&-a_{2}^{\prime}\beta_{13}^{\prime}+\gamma_{13}^{\prime}b_{2}^{\prime}\\
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{1}\text{ is generated by }&a_{2}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime}\\
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{12,3}\text{ is generated by }&a_{2}^{\prime}-b_{2}^{\prime},\beta_{13}^{\prime}-\gamma_{13}^{\prime}\\
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{13,2}\text{ is generated by }&a_{2}^{\prime},\gamma_{13}^{\prime}\\
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{23,1}\text{ is generated by }&b_{2}^{\prime},\beta_{13}^{\prime}\\
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{2}\text{ is generated by }&\beta_{13}^{\prime},\gamma_{13}^{\prime}\end{cases}.

A.3.6.
Blowup

We blow up the ideal
\langle a_{2}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime}\rangle
of
\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{1}
and verify in local coordinates that the obtained pair
(\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{1},\bm{\mathrm{D}}^{1})
is, as has been pointed out in
[
RS84
]
, smooth. Also we calculate the invariant gauge form in this smooth pair.

Naturally
\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{1}}(\bm{\mathrm{O}})
is the union of two affine open subvarieties
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{1}\cup\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{2}
.
For simplicity, coordinates
a_{3},c_{2},\alpha_{13}
are omitted below.

A.3.7.
Blowup,
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{1}

Here we have coordinates
\beta_{13}^{\prime},\gamma_{13}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{b}_{2}
with

b_{2}^{\prime}=a_{2}^{\prime}\widetilde{b}_{2}
. And the equation defining
\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{1}}(\bm{\mathrm{O}})
is
\beta_{13}^{\prime}=\gamma_{13}^{\prime}\widetilde{b}_{2}
. Thus we can eliminate
\beta_{13}^{\prime}
and treat
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{1}
as
\Spec\mathbb{Q}[\gamma_{13}^{\prime},a_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{b}_{2}]
.

One can compute that (let
\bm{\mathrm{E}}
denote the exceptional divisor)

1.

\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}}=\pm{(a_{2}^{\prime})^{-9}(1-\widetilde{b}_{2})^{-6}(\gamma_{13}^{\prime})^{-9}(\widetilde{b}_{2})^{-6}}\cdot{\mathrm{d}a_{2}^{\prime}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{b}_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d\gamma}^{\prime}_{13}}
;

2.

\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{12,3}^{+}=\{1-\widetilde{b}_{2}=0\},\,\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{13,2}^{+}\text{ is in the complement},\,\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{23,1}^{+}=\{\widetilde{b}_{2}=0\}
;

3.

\bm{\mathrm{E}}=\{a_{2}^{\prime}=0\},\,(\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{2})^{+}=\{\gamma_{13}^{\prime}=0\}
.

A.3.8.
Blowup,
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{2}

Here we have coordinates
\beta_{13}^{\prime},\gamma_{13}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{a}_{2}
with

a_{2}^{\prime}=b_{2}^{\prime}\widetilde{a}_{2}
. And the equation defining
\Bl_{\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{1}}(\bm{\mathrm{O}})
in
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{1}
is
\gamma_{13}^{\prime}=\beta_{13}^{\prime}\widetilde{a}_{2}
. Thus we can eliminate
\gamma_{13}^{\prime}
and treat
\bm{\mathrm{U}}_{2}
as
\Spec\mathbb{Q}[\beta_{13}^{\prime},b_{2}^{\prime},\widetilde{a}_{2}]
.

One can compute that (let
\bm{\mathrm{E}}
denote the exceptional divisor)

1.

\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}}=\pm{(b_{2}^{\prime})^{-9}(1-\widetilde{a}_{2})^{-6}(\beta_{13}^{\prime})^{-9}(\widetilde{a}_{2})^{-6}}\cdot{\mathrm{d}b_{2}^{\prime}\wedge\mathrm{d}\widetilde{a}_{2}\wedge\mathrm{d\beta}^{\prime}_{13}}
;

2.

\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{12,3}^{+}=\{1-\widetilde{a}_{2}=0\},\,\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{13,2}^{+}=\{\widetilde{a}_{2}=0\},\,\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{23,1}^{+}\text{ is in the complement}
;

3.

\bm{\mathrm{E}}=\{b_{2}^{\prime}=0\},\,(\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{2})^{+}=\{\beta_{13}^{\prime}=0\}
.

A.3.9.
The divisor of the invariant gauge form

Labeling the boundary divisors on
\bm{\mathrm{X}}^{2}
as

\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}:=\bm{\mathrm{E}},\;\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{2}:=\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{12,3}^{+},\;\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{3}:=\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{13,2}^{+},\;\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{4}:=\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{23,1}^{+},\;\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{5}:=(\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{123}^{2})^{+}.

From the description above,
one gets

-\divisor(\omega_{\bm{\mathrm{M}}_{3}})=9\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{1}+6\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{2}+6\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{3}+6\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{4}+9\bm{\mathrm{D}}_{5}.

A.3.10.
Mapping to the measure compactification space

Let
\Gamma\leq\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q})
be an arithmetic subgroup.
We have the following, implied by
[
SZ19
, Theorem 2.4]
.

Theorem A.8
.

Let
(g_{n})
be a sequence in
\mathrm{G}
.

1.

If
(g_{n})
is unbounded modulo
\mathrm{H}_{ij}
for every
\{i,j\}\subset\{1,2,3\}
, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left[(g_{n})_{*}\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{H}]}\right]=\left[\mathrm{m}_{[\bm{\mathrm{G}}]}\right];

2.

If
(g_{n})
is unbounded modulo
\mathrm{H}
but convergent to
\delta\in\mathrm{G}
modulo
\mathrm{H}_{ij}
for some (necessarily unique)
\{i,j\}
, then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\left[(g_{n})_{*}\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{H}]}\right]=\left[\delta_{*}\mathrm{m}_{[\mathrm{H}_{ij}]}\right].

As a corollary, we have

Corollary A.9
.

Let
X^{\meas}
be the closure of
\{\alpha^{\psi}_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{m}^{\psi}_{[\mathrm{H}]})\}
in
\Prob^{\psi}(\mathrm{G}/\Gamma)
. Then

X^{\meas}=\alpha^{\psi}_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{m}^{\psi}_{[\mathrm{H}]})\bigsqcup\left(\alpha^{\psi}_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{m}^{\psi}_{[\mathrm{H}_{12}]})\cup\alpha^{\psi}_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{m}^{\psi}_{[\mathrm{H}_{13}]})\cup\alpha^{\psi}_{\mathrm{G}}(\mathrm{m}^{\psi}_{[\mathrm{H}_{23}]})\right)\bigsqcup\left\{\mathrm{m}^{\psi}_{[\bm{\mathrm{G}}]}\right\}.

Appendix B
Divergence of Translates of Homogeneous Closed Subsets

Let
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
be a connected linear algebraic group over
\mathbb{Q}
,
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
be a connected observable
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup, and
\Gamma
be an arithmetic subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
. Fix a maximal reductive connected
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\red}
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and hence
\bm{\mathrm{G}}=\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\red}\ltimes\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
. Also fix a Cartan involution and hence a maximal compact subgroup
\mathrm{K}
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\red}(\mathbb{R})
.
Depending on this choice, each parabolic
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{P}}
is associated with a
\mathbb{Q}
-split subtorus
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K}}
that is isomorphic to
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}}
under
p^{\spl}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}}
.
Unlike the main body of the paper, in this appendix, the Roman letter
\mathrm{L}
is used to denote
\bm{\mathrm{L}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}
for an algebraic group over
\mathbb{R}
.

Let
\Phi^{\red}(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{P}})
be the nontrivial characters of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K}}
appearing in
\mathfrak{r}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\mathfrak{p})/\mathfrak{r}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\mathfrak{g})\subset\mathfrak{g}/\mathfrak{r}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\mathfrak{g})
. Let
\Delta^{\red}(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{P}})
be the subset of cardinality
\dim\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K}}
whose
\mathbb{Z}_{\geq 0}
-span equals to
\Phi^{\red}(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{P}})
. When
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is already reductive, the
superscript “
{\red}
” is dropped.

Theorem B.1
.

Given a sequence
(g_{n})\subset\mathrm{G}
, after passing to a subsequence, there exist a sequence
(h_{n})\subset\mathrm{H}
,
(\gamma_{n})\subset\Gamma
and a parabolic
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{P}}
such that the following holds. Write
g_{n}h_{n}\gamma_{n}^{-1}=k_{n}a_{n}p_{n}
using horospherical coordinates of
(\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K})
.
Then

(1)

(p_{n})
is bounded;

(2)

\alpha(a_{n})\to 0
for every
\alpha\in\Delta^{\red}(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{P}})
;

(3)

if
(a_{n})
is unbounded, there exist a
\mathbb{Q}
-representation
\bm{\mathrm{V}}
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
factoring through
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
and
\bm{\mathrm{v}}\in\bm{\mathrm{V}}(\mathbb{Q})
such that the line spanned by
\bm{\mathrm{v}}
is preserved by
\bm{\mathrm{P}}
,
\bm{\mathrm{v}}
is fixed by
\gamma_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\gamma_{n}^{-1}
for all
n
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}.\bm{\mathrm{v}}=\bm{\mathrm{0}}
;

(4)

(\gamma_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\gamma_{n}^{-1})
strongly converges to some observable subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.

Compared to
[
Zha22
, Theorem 5.2]
, 3 and 4 are new. Note that whereas one quotients
\Gamma
from the left in
[
Zha22
]
, here we choose to quotient from the right. Also, when one has
\alpha(a_{n})\to\infty
in
[
Zha22
]
, we have
\alpha(a_{n})\to 0
here.
We shall follow the logic of
[
Zha22
]
by first reducing to the case of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}=\SL_{N}
. And this special case will be handled with the help of “canonical polygons”. The proof is independent from the work of
[
DGU23
]
and can actually be used to give an alternative treatment of their main results. The connection between canonical polygons and nondivergence property of unipotent flows has been noted in
[
dS23
]
.

For simplicity, by saying the
(\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K})
coordinate of some element, we mean its
horospherical coordinate
associated to
(\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K})
(see
[
Zha22
, Lemma 2.1]
,
[
BJ06
, Chapter 9]
or
[
BS73
, Proposition 1.5]
).

B.1.
Reduction to the reductive case

We explain how the general case follows from the case when
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is reductive.
Indeed, by projecting everything to
p^{\red}:\bm{\mathrm{G}}\to\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
, after passing to a subsequence, we find

(h_{n})\subset\mathrm{H}
,
(\lambda_{n})\subset\Gamma
and a parabolic
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{P}}
such that if
g_{n}h_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}=k_{n}a_{n}q_{n}
is the
(\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K})
coordinate of
g_{n}h_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}
, then

1.

q_{n}=b_{n}u_{n}
for some
(b_{n})
bounded in
\mathrm{P}
and
(u_{n})\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})(\mathbb{R})
.

2.

\alpha(a_{n})\to 0
for every
\alpha\in\Delta^{\red}(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{P}})
;

3.

there exist a
\mathbb{Q}
-representation
\bm{\mathrm{V}}
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
factoring through
p^{\red}
and
\bm{\mathrm{v}}\in\bm{\mathrm{V}}(\mathbb{Q})
such that the line spanned by
\bm{\mathrm{v}}

is preserved by
\bm{\mathrm{P}}
,
\bm{\mathrm{v}}
is fixed by
\gamma_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\gamma_{n}^{-1}
for all
n
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}a_{n}.\bm{\mathrm{v}}=\bm{\mathrm{0}}
;

4.

\left(p^{\red}\left(\lambda_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\lambda_{n}^{-1}\right)\right)
strongly converges to some
observable subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
.

Write
u_{n}=b_{n}^{\prime}\lambda_{n}^{\prime}
for some bounded sequence
(b_{n}^{\prime})\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})(\mathbb{R})
and
(\lambda_{n}^{\prime})\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})(\mathbb{R})\cap\Gamma
. Let
\gamma_{n}:=\lambda_{n}^{\prime}\lambda_{n}
. It only remains to verify item 4. in the theorem. Since
(\lambda^{\prime}_{n})
are contained in the kernel of
p^{\red}
, we still have
\left(p^{\red}\left(\gamma_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\gamma_{n}^{-1}\right)\right)
strongly converges to some observable subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{F}}
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}/\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
. After passing to a subsequence assume
(\gamma_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\gamma_{n}^{-1})
strongly converges to
\bm{\mathrm{L}}
. Then
p^{\red}(\bm{\mathrm{L}})=\bm{\mathrm{F}}
. Note that
(p^{\red})^{-1}(\bm{\mathrm{F}})
is observable in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
by assumption. On the other hand,
(p^{\red})^{-1}(\bm{\mathrm{F}})=\bm{\mathrm{L}}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
and hence
(p^{\red})^{-1}(\bm{\mathrm{F}})/\bm{\mathrm{L}}\cong\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})/\bm{\mathrm{U}}
for some
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{U}}
of
\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
. Therefore,
\bm{\mathrm{L}}
is observable in
(p^{\red})^{-1}(\bm{\mathrm{F}})
and hence in
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
. This completes the proof.

B.2.
Reduction to the
SL_{N}
case

Let
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
be a connected reductive linear algebraic group over
\mathbb{Q}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup of some
\SL_{N}
that is invariant under taking transpose. We let
\mathrm{K}:=\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})\cap\SO_{N}(\mathbb{R})
be the maximal compact subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})
that is transpose invariant.

The concept of Siegel sets can be generalized to reductive groups as follows. Write
\bm{\mathrm{G}}=\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\semisimple}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})^{\an}\cdot\bm{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})^{\spl}
. Then every Siegel set
\mathfrak{S}
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
is of the form
\mathfrak{S}^{\prime}\cdot F\cdot\bm{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})^{\spl}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}
where
\mathfrak{S}\subset\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\semisimple}(\mathbb{R})
is a Siegel set for
\bm{\mathrm{G}}^{\semisimple}
and
F\subset\bm{\mathrm{Z}}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})^{\an}(\mathbb{R})
is a compact subset that is left invariant under the maximal compact subtorus.
For every
\mathbb{Q}
-minimal parabolic subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{P}}
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
, there exist finitely many
c_{1},...,c_{l}\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q})
and a Siegel set
\mathfrak{S}
associated with
(\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K})
such that

\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})=\bigcup_{i=1}^{l}\mathfrak{S}\cdot c_{i}\cdot\Gamma.

The main argument of
[
Zha22
, Section 5]
carries through. We sketch the proof below.
Without loss of generality, assume
\Gamma=\SL_{N}(\mathbb{Z})\cap\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})
.

By the
\SL_{N}
case, after passing to a subsequence, there are
(h_{n})\subset\mathrm{H}
,
(\gamma_{n})\subset\SL_{N}(\mathbb{Z})
and a parabolic
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}\subset\SL_{N}
such that if the
(\bm{\mathrm{Q}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
coordinate (for simplicity we write
\mathrm{K}^{\prime}:=\SO_{N}(\mathbb{R})
) of
g_{n}h_{n}\gamma_{n}^{-1}
is
k_{n}a_{n}q_{n}
, then

1.

(q_{n})
is bounded;

2.

\alpha(a_{n})\to 0
for every
\alpha\in\Delta(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{Q}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime}},\bm{\mathrm{Q}})
;

3.

\gamma_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\gamma_{n}^{-1}\subset\bm{\mathrm{Q}}
for all
n
. And there exist a
\SL_{N}
-representation and a
\mathbb{Q}
-vector
\bm{\mathrm{v}}
fixed by
\gamma_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\gamma_{n}^{-1}
and the line spanned by
\bm{\mathrm{v}}
is preserved by
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}
with a nontrivial character;

4.

(\gamma_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\gamma_{n}^{-1})
strongly converges to an observable subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{L}}
of
\SL_{N}
.

Note that
a_{n}.\bm{\mathrm{v}}\to\bm{\mathrm{0}}
follows automatically.

B.2.1.
Step 1

Assume
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}(\mathbb{Z})\cap\Lie(\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}))=\oplus\mathbb{Z}w_{i}^{\bm{\mathrm{Q}}}
and let
\bm{\mathrm{w}}:=\oplus w_{i}^{\bm{\mathrm{Q}}}
. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}g_{n}h_{n}\gamma_{n}^{-1}.\bm{\mathrm{w}}=\bm{\mathrm{0}}.

By geometric invariant theory, there exist
n_{0}\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}
and
\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}\in\mathfrak{X}^{\mathbb{Q}}_{*}(\bm{\mathrm{G}})
such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}\gamma_{n_{0}}^{-1}.\bm{\mathrm{w}}=0.

Let (similar notations are also used for cocharacters other than
\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}
)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in\SL_{N}\;\middle|\;\lim_{t\to\infty}\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}x\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}^{-1}\text{ exists}\right\};

\displaystyle\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}}

\displaystyle:=\left\{x\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}\;\middle|\;\lim_{t\to\infty}\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}x\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}^{-1}\text{ exists}\right\}.

Then
\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}})
contains
\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\gamma_{n_{0}}^{-1}\bm{\mathrm{Q}}\gamma_{n_{0}})
, or equivalently,
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}}\subset\gamma_{n_{0}}^{-1}\bm{\mathrm{Q}}\gamma_{n_{0}}
. Replacing
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}
by
\gamma_{n_{0}}^{-1}\bm{\mathrm{Q}}\gamma_{n_{0}}
and
\gamma_{n}
by
\gamma_{n_{0}}^{-1}\gamma_{n}
, we assume without loss of generality that
\gamma_{n_{0}}
is the identity element.

B.2.2.
Step 2

Let
\bm{\mathrm{L}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}}
(resp.
\bm{\mathrm{L}}_{\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime}}
) be the maximal reductive subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}}
(resp.
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}}
) that is invariant under taking transpose.
Let
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}}
be the central torus of
\bm{\mathrm{L}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}}
, which is conjugate to a maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-split torus
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\max}
in
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}}
.
We have
\bm{\mathrm{L}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{L}}_{\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}})\subset\bm{\mathrm{R}}_{\bm{\mathrm{u}}}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}})
.
There exists
\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}\in\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\max})
such that

1.

the centralizer of
\{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}\}
in
\SL_{N}
is equal to the centralizer of
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\max}
in
\SL_{N}
. In particular
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-minimal parabolic subgroup of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
;

2.

\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}}
. In particular,
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{a}}_{t}}
.

Thus
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}}
is contained in
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime}}
.
Consequently, the
(\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K})
coordinate of
g\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{R})
is the same as its
(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
coordinate.

B.2.3.
Step 3

Because
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}}
is
\mathbb{Q}
-minimal, by passing to a subsequence, there exist
c_{1}\in\bm{\mathrm{G}}(\mathbb{Q})
,
(\lambda_{n})\subset\Gamma
and a Siegel set
\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}})
associated to
(\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K})
such that

g_{n}h_{n}\in\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}})c_{1}\lambda_{n},\quad\forall\,n\in\mathbb{Z}^{+}.

In particular, if
g_{n}h_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}c_{1}^{-1}=k_{n}^{\prime}a_{n}^{\prime}p_{n}^{\prime}
under
(\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K})
coordinate, then
(p_{n}^{\prime})
is bounded and for some
t_{0}>0
, for every
n
and
\alpha\in\Delta(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}})
, one has
\alpha(a_{n}^{\prime})<t_{0}
.

Let
\Phi(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime}},\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
be the collection of nontrivial weights of
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime}}
appearing in the adjoint action on
\mathfrak{sl}_{N}
.
Passing to a subsequence, assume that for every
\beta\in\Phi(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime}},\mathfrak{sl}_{N})
, the sequence
\left(\beta(a_{n}^{\prime})\right)
is bounded (away from
0
and
+\infty
), or converges to
0
, or diverges to
+\infty
.
Define a parabolic
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup by

\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{(a_{n}^{\prime})}:=\left\{x\in\SL_{N}\;\middle|\;\left(a_{n}^{\prime}xa_{n}^{\prime-1}\right)\text{ is bounded}\right\}.

One notes that
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}}
is contained in
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{(a_{n}^{\prime})}
.

Since
(a_{n}^{\prime})
is a sequence in
\mathrm{A}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}}
, which is conjugate to
\mathrm{S}_{\max}
in
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}}
, we can
choose
\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime}_{t}\in\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\max})
such that

\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{(a_{n}^{\prime})},\;\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime}_{t}}=\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}},\;\bm{\mathrm{Z}}_{\SL_{N}}(\{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime}\})=\bm{\mathrm{Z}}_{\SL_{N}}(\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\max}).

Then the
(\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}}=\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime}_{t}},\mathrm{K})
coordinate of
g_{n}h_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}c_{1}^{-1}
is the same as its
(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime}_{t}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
coordinate. From the definition of
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{(a^{\prime}_{n})}
, one deduces that for any weight
\alpha\in\Phi(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime}_{t}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{(a^{\prime}_{n})})
and in particular
\alpha\in\Phi(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime}_{t}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime}})
, we have
\alpha(a_{n}^{\prime})<t_{1}
for some
t_{1}>0
and for all
n
.
We conclude from here that
g_{n}h_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}c_{1}^{-1}
is contained in a Siegel set
\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime}})
associated to
(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
.

B.2.4.
Step 4

Though
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}}=\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime}_{t}}
, it is not clear whether
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime}_{t}}
contain a common Borel subgroup.
Find
w_{1}\in\SL_{N}(\mathbb{Q})
such that
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime}:=w_{1}\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime}}w_{1}^{-1}\cap\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}}
is a parabolic subgroup. Thus
g_{n}h_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}c_{1}^{-1}w_{1}^{-1}
is contained in some
Siegel set of
(w_{1}\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime}_{t}}w_{1}^{-1},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
and hence some
Siegel set
\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime})
associated to
(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
.
On the other hand,
(g_{n}h_{n}\gamma_{n}^{-1})
is known to be contained in some Siegel set attached to
(\bm{\mathrm{Q}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
and hence in some Siegel set
\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime})^{\prime}
attached to
(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
.
Replacing by a larger Siegel set if necessary, assume
\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime})=\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime})^{\prime}
. Hence

g_{n}h_{n}\gamma_{n}^{-1}\in\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime})\cap\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime})w_{1}c_{1}\lambda_{n}\gamma_{n}^{-1}.

In particular, this intersection is nonempty. But for every
q\in\SL_{N}(\mathbb{Q})
, the set
\{\gamma\in\SL_{N}(\mathbb{Z}),\;\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime})\cap\mathfrak{S}(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime})q\gamma\}
is finite. Therefore, after passing to a subsequence, there exists
\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\in\SL_{N}(\mathbb{Z})
such that
\lambda_{n}\gamma_{n}^{-1}=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}
for all
n
.

B.2.5.
Step 5

Let
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime\prime}:=\gamma_{1}^{\prime}\bm{\mathrm{Q}}\gamma_{1}^{\prime-1}
, which contains
\lambda_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\lambda_{n}^{-1}
for all
n
. As in Step 1 and 2, find some maximal
\mathbb{Q}
-split torus
\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\max}^{\prime}
of
\bm{\mathrm{G}}
and
\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\max}^{\prime})
such that

1.

\bm{\mathrm{Z}}_{\SL_{N}}(\{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime\prime}\})=\bm{\mathrm{Z}}_{\SL_{N}}(\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\max}^{\prime})
. In particular,
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime\prime}_{t}}
is a
\mathbb{Q}
-minimal parabolic subgroup;

2.

\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime\prime}}\subset\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime\prime}
.

By assumption,
g_{n}h_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}=g_{n}h_{n}\gamma_{n}^{-1}\gamma_{1}^{\prime-1}
belongs to some Siegel set associated to
(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime\prime},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
and hence to
(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime\prime}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
. Let

g_{n}h_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}=k_{n}^{\prime\prime}a_{n}^{\prime\prime}p_{n}^{\prime\prime}

be the
(\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime\prime}_{t}},\mathrm{K})
coordinate, which is the same as the
(\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime\prime}_{t}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime})
coordinate.
We find that

1.

(p_{n}^{\prime\prime})
is bounded;

2.

there exists
t_{2}>0
such that for every
n
and
\alpha\in\Delta(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime\prime}_{t}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime\prime}_{t}})
, we have
\alpha(a_{n}^{\prime\prime})<t_{2}
;

3.

\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime\prime}=\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{(g_{n}h_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1})}=\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{(a_{n}^{\prime\prime})}
.

Choose
\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}\in\mathfrak{X}_{*}^{\mathbb{Q}}(\bm{\mathrm{S}}_{\max}^{\prime})
such that
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{(a_{n}^{\prime\prime})}=\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}}
.
After passing to a subsequence, assume that for every
\alpha\in\Delta(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime\prime}_{t}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}^{\prime\prime}_{t}})
, either
(\alpha(a_{n}^{\prime\prime}))
converges to
0
or is bounded away from
0
.
Let
I^{\prime\prime}
be those
\alpha
such that
(\alpha(a_{n}^{\prime\prime}))
is bounded away from
0
. Then

(\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{b}}_{t}^{\prime\prime}})_{I^{\prime\prime}}=\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}}=\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}}\cap\bm{\mathrm{G}}=\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime\prime}\cap\bm{\mathrm{G}}
.
In particular,
\lambda_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\lambda_{n}^{-1}
is contained in
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}}
for all
n
. Also, if
g_{n}h_{n}\lambda_{n}^{-1}=k_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}a_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}p_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime}
is the
(\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}},\mathrm{K})
coordinate, then
(p_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime})
is bounded and
\alpha(a_{n}^{\prime\prime\prime})\to 0
for all
\alpha\in\Delta(\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}},\mathrm{K}},\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}})
.

Finally, by assumption, there exist certain
\SL_{N}
-representation and a
\mathbb{Q}
vector
\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\prime\prime}
fixed by
\lambda_{n}\bm{\mathrm{H}}\lambda_{n}^{-1}
and the line spanned by
\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\prime\prime}
is preserved by
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime\prime}
with a nontrivial character, which must be nontrivial on
\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}
since
\bm{\mathrm{Q}}^{\prime\prime}=\bm{\mathrm{Q}}_{\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}}
. In particular,
\bm{\mathrm{v}}^{\prime\prime}
is preserved by
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\bm{\mathrm{c}}_{t}}
with a nontrivial character. So we are done.

B.3.
The case of
SL_{N}

Fix an observable
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
of
\SL_{N}
and a sequence
(g_{n})\subset\SL_{N}(\mathbb{R})
. We prove Theorem
B.1
in this case using the idea of canonical polygons (see
[
Cas04
,
Gra84
]
). For simplicity we abbreviate
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}},\mathrm{K}^{\prime}}
as
\bm{\mathrm{A}}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}}
below and continue call
\mathrm{K}^{\prime}:=\SO_{N}(\mathbb{R})
.

B.3.1.
Lattice flags

A subgroup
\Delta
of
\mathbb{Z}^{N}
is said to be primitive iff
(\Delta\otimes\mathbb{Q})\cap\mathbb{Z}^{N}=\Delta
.
Let
\Prim(\mathbb{Z}^{N})
be the collection of all primitive subgroups of
\mathbb{Z}^{N}
.
For a
\mathbb{Q}
-character
\alpha
of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
, let
\Prim^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})
denote those
\Delta=\mathbb{Z}\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{1}\oplus...\oplus\mathbb{Z}\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{k}
with

h.\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{1}\wedge...\wedge\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{k}=\alpha(h)\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{1}\wedge...\wedge\bm{\mathrm{v}}_{k},\quad\forall\,h\in\mathrm{H}.

Let
\Prim^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})
be the union of these
\Prim^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})
’s.

A totally ordered subset of
\Prim(\mathbb{Z}^{N})
(without loss of generality, assumed to contain
\{\bm{\mathrm{0}}\}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{N}
) is referred to as a
lattice flag
, written as

\mathcal{F}=\left\{\{\bm{\mathrm{0}}\}=\Delta_{0}\subsetneq\Delta_{1}\subsetneq...\subsetneq\Delta_{l}=\mathbb{Z}^{N}\right\}.

The number
l-1
is called the rank of
\mathcal{F}
.
For
i=1,...,l
, we define

(47)

\overline{\Delta_{i}}:=\left(\Delta_{i}/\Delta_{i-1}\right)^{{}^{\prime}}:=\left(\frac{\left\lVert\Delta_{i}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert\Delta_{i-1}\right\rVert}\right)^{-\frac{1}{\rank(\Delta_{i})-\rank(\Delta_{i-1})}}\cdot\Delta_{i}/\Delta_{i-1},

which has covolume one in the quotient Euclidean space
\Delta_{i}\otimes\mathbb{R}/\Delta_{i-1}\otimes\mathbb{R}
.
We also define a parabolic
\mathbb{Q}
-subgroup by

\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathcal{F}}:=\left\{g\in\SL_{N}(\mathbb{C})\;\middle|\;g\text{ preserves }\Delta_{i}\otimes\mathbb{C},\;\forall\,i\right\}.

Given a subset
\mathcal{I}
of
\{0,1,...,N\}
containing
\{0,N\}
, ordered as

\mathcal{I}=\left\{0=i_{0}<i_{1}<...<i_{l}=N\right\}
,
let
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}}
be the
standard flag
associated to
\mathcal{I}
defined by

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}}:=\left\{\{\bm{\mathrm{0}}\}\subsetneq\oplus_{i=1}^{i_{1}}\mathbb{Z}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{i}\subsetneq...\subsetneq\oplus_{i=1}^{i_{l-1}}\mathbb{Z}\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{i}\subsetneq\mathbb{Z}^{N}\right\}.

For simplicity we write
\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathcal{I}}:=\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{I}}}
.

B.3.2.
Quotient lattices

Fix some
\mathcal{I}=\left\{0=i_{0}<i_{1}<...<i_{l}=N\right\}
for this subsection.

Define, for
k=1,...,l
,

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k}^{\Std}:=\bigoplus_{i=1}^{i_{k}}\mathbb{Z}.\bm{\mathrm{e}}_{i},\quad V_{k}:=\Lambda_{k}^{\Std}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{R},\quad\overline{V}_{k}:=V_{k}/V_{k-1},\quad\overline{V}_{k}(\mathbb{Z}):=\Lambda_{k}^{\Std}/\Lambda_{k-1}^{\Std},

and
\pi_{k}:\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{I}}:=\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathcal{I}}(\mathbb{R})^{\circ}\to\SL(\overline{V}_{k})
by

p\mapsto c_{p}\cdot\left(p|_{V_{k}}\;(\mathrm{mod}\;V_{k-1})\right)

where
c_{p}
is the unique positive real number such that the right hand side has determinant one.
\pi_{k}
descends to a continuous map

\overline{\pi}_{k}:\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{I}}/\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\SL_{N}(\mathbb{Z})\to\SL(\overline{V}_{k})/\SL(\overline{V}_{k}(\mathbb{Z})).

that is equivariant with respect to
\pi_{k}
.
The natural map

p\mapsto\left\{\{\bm{\mathrm{0}}\}=p\Lambda_{0}^{\Std}\subsetneq p\Lambda_{1}^{\Std}\subsetneq...\subsetneq p\Lambda_{l}^{\Std}=\mathbb{Z}^{N}\right\}

induces a bijection between

\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{I}}/\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\SL_{N}(\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathrm{LF}_{\mathcal{I}}:=\left\{(\Delta_{\star})\text{ is a lattice flag }\;\middle|\;(\Delta_{i})_{\mathbb{R}}=V_{i},\,\forall\,i=1,...,l\right\}.

If we additionally identity

\SL(\overline{V}_{k})/\SL(\overline{V}_{k}(\mathbb{Z}))\cong\left\{\text{ unimodular lattices in }\overline{V}_{k}\,\right\}

by
g\mapsto g\overline{V}_{k}(\mathbb{Z})
,
then
\overline{\pi}_{k}
is nothing but

(\Delta_{\star})\mapsto\overline{\Delta}_{k}

defined in last subsection (see Equa.(
47
)).

Let
\overline{p}^{\spl}:\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{I}}/\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\SL_{N}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathrm{S}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathcal{I}}}
. It is direct to check the following

Lemma B.2
.

For each
\mathcal{I}
, the map

\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{I}}/\mathrm{P}_{\mathcal{I}}\cap\SL_{N}(\mathbb{Z})\to\mathrm{S}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\times\prod_{k=1}^{l}\SL(V_{k})/\SL(V_{k}(\mathbb{Z}))
defined by
\Phi_{\mathcal{I}}:=\overline{p}^{\spl}\times\prod_{k}\overline{\pi}_{k}
is a proper continuous map.

For
k=1,...,l
, define
j_{k}:=i_{k}-i_{k-1}
and

f_{k},\varphi_{k},\alpha_{k}:\mathrm{A}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathcal{I}}}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
by

f_{k}(a):=\left\lVert a\Lambda^{\Std}_{k}\right\rVert,\quad\varphi_{k}(a):=\left(\frac{\left\lVert a\Lambda^{\Std}_{k}\right\rVert}{\left\lVert a\Lambda^{\Std}_{k-1}\right\rVert}\right)^{\frac{1}{j_{k}}},\quad\alpha_{k}(a):=\varphi_{k}(a)/\varphi_{k+1}(a).

One can check that

(48)

\Delta(\mathrm{A}_{\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathcal{I}}},\bm{\mathrm{P}}_{\mathcal{I}})=\left\{\alpha_{k}\;\middle|\;k=1,...,l-1\right\}.

B.3.3.
Canonical flags

In this subsection we fix an element
g\in\SL_{N}(\mathbb{R})
.
Define

\displaystyle\Plot^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g)

\displaystyle:=\left\{\left(\rank(\Delta),\log(\left\lVert g\Delta\right\rVert)\right)\;\middle|\;\Delta\in\Prim^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})\right\};

\displaystyle\Poly^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g)

\displaystyle:=\text{closure of the convex hull of }\Plot^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g).

\Poly^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g)
is a polygon of finitely many sides. Let

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g):=\left\{\Delta\in\Prim^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})\;\middle|\;\left(\rank\Delta,\log(\left\lVert g\Delta\right\rVert)\right)\text{ is an extreme point of }\Poly^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g)\right\}.

Lemma B.3
.

For every
g\in\SL_{N}(\mathbb{R})
,
\mathcal{E}^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g)
is a lattice flag containing
\{\bm{\mathrm{0}}\}
and
\mathbb{Z}^{N}
.

A proof can be found in
[
Cas04
, Section 4]
. Let
r_{g}:=\rank(\mathcal{E}^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g))
and write

\mathcal{E}^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g)=\left\{\{\bm{\mathrm{0}}\}=\Delta_{0}(g)\subsetneq\Delta_{1}(g)\subsetneq...\subsetneq\Delta_{r_{g}+1}(g)=\mathbb{Z}^{N}\right\},

called the
canonical flag
. Define

\displaystyle\type(g)

\displaystyle:=\left\{0=\rank(\Delta_{0}(g))<\rank(\Delta_{1}(g))<...<\rank(\Delta_{r_{g}+1}(g))=N\right\};

\displaystyle\slope_{k}(g)

\displaystyle:=\frac{\log\left\lVert(g\Delta_{k}(g)\right\rVert)-\log(\left\lVert g\Delta_{k-1}(g)\right\rVert)}{\rank(\Delta_{k}(g))-\rank(\Delta_{k-1}(g))},\;k=1,...,r_{g}+1;

\displaystyle d_{k}(g)

\displaystyle:=\slope_{k+1}(g)-\slope_{k}(g),\;k=1,...,r_{g}.

B.3.4.
Variation of canonical flags

Fixing some
g\in\SL_{N}(\mathbb{R})
, we would like to search for a better one among
\mathcal{E}^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(gh)
as
h
varies in
\mathrm{H}
by (locally) optimizing certain quantities. This part is an extra key step required to treat general connected observable subgroups instead of just those without nontrivial
\mathbb{Q}
-characters.

Find a
\mathbb{Q}
-split subtorus
\bm{\mathrm{S}}^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
such that
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
is an almost direct product of
({}^{\circ}\bm{\mathrm{H}})^{\circ}
and
\bm{\mathrm{S}}^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}
. Thus the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{h}={}^{\circ}\mathfrak{h}\oplus\mathfrak{s}^{\mathfrak{h}}
. Write
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathbb{R}}
for the
\mathbb{R}
-span of
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathfrak{h}}
. For a
\mathbb{Q}
-character
\alpha
of
\bm{\mathrm{H}}
, we regard
\mathrm{d}\alpha
as a linear functional on
\mathfrak{s}^{\mathfrak{h}}_{\mathbb{R}}
since it vanishes on
{}^{\circ}\mathfrak{h}_{\mathbb{R}}
.
Let
\Phi_{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}^{\Std}
collect all
\mathrm{d\alpha}
such that
\Prim^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}_{\alpha}(\mathbb{Z}^{N})
is nonzero.

For
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, let
\CVX_{\lambda}
be the collection of all convex closed polygons
\mathcal{P}
such that

1.

[0,N]\times\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\subset\mathcal{P}\subset\{(x,y),\;y\geq\lambda,\,0\leq x\leq N\}
;

2.

(x,y)\in\Extre(\mathcal{P})\implies x\in\{0,1,...,N\}
and
\{(0,0),(N,0)\}\subset\Extre(\mathcal{P})
.

Equip
\cup\CVX_{\lambda}
with the Chabauty topology.
Note that
\Poly^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g)
belongs to
\CVX_{\lambda}
for some
\lambda
.
Given some
\mathcal{P}\in\CVX_{\lambda}
for some
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, we let

1.

y_{i}(\mathcal{P}):=\inf\left\{y\;\middle|\;(i,y)\in\mathcal{P}\right\}
for
i\in\{0,...,N\}
. So
y_{0}=y_{N}=0
;

2.

s_{i}(\mathcal{P}):=y_{i}(\mathcal{P})-y_{i-1}(\mathcal{P})
for
i\in\{1,...,N\}
;

3.

d_{i}(\mathcal{P})=s_{i+1}(\mathcal{P})-s_{i}(\mathcal{P})=y_{i+1}(\mathcal{P})+y_{i-1}(\mathcal{P})-2y_{i}(\mathcal{P})
for
i\in\{1,...,N-1\}
.

Thus
d_{i}(g)=d_{\rank(\Delta_{i}(g))}(\Poly^{\bm{\mathrm{H}}}(g))
.
Also let
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}
be a permutation on
\{1,...,N-1\}
such that

1.

\left\{d_{i}(\mathcal{P})\;\middle|\;i=1,...,N-1\right\}=\left\{d_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}(1)}(\mathcal{P})\geq d_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}(2)}(\mathcal{P})\geq....\geq d_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}(N-1)}(\mathcal{P})\right\}
;

2.

if
d_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}(i)}(\mathcal{P})=d_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}(i+1)}(\mathcal{P})
then
\tau_{\mathcal{P}}(i)<\tau_{\mathcal{P}}(i+1)
.

Define a partial order
\prec
on
\cup\CVX_{\lambda}
by
\mathcal{P}\prec\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
iff
\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}^{\prime}
or

y_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}}(m)}(\mathcal{P})<y_{\tau_{\mathcal{P}^{\prime}}(m)}(\mathcal{P}^{\prime})
with
